===PAGE 3=== (plain, 29 lines)
   1| 3
   2| In Memoriam
   3|         Jean Elizabeth Hobbs, a dedicated and energetic member of the Lunenburg community for
   4| more than 60 years. She graduated from Northbridge High School in 1944 and went on to study at Memorial Hospital
   5| School of Nursing in Worcester, graduating in 1947. That same year she married Frederick B. Hobbs and moved to Lunen-
   6| burg, where he would later become Police Chief.
   7| She was a contributor to the town history, Lunenburg: The Heritage of Turkey Hills, 1728-1978 and played key roles in
   8| planning the town's 250th and 275th anniversary celebrations.
   9| In the 1960s, she was a Girl Scout leader, Den Mother, and a founding member of the Lunenburg Music Aiders. She served
  10| on the Town's 1976 Bicentennial Committee, the Insurance Advisory Committee, Board of Directors for the Memorial Fund
  11| and for School Community Linked Services and was a member of the Lunenburg Historical Society. She was a fixture at
  12| Lunenburg's Annual Town Meeting, never reluctant to share her thoughts and opinions.
  13| Jean was dedicated to elder services. She was Chairwoman of the Lunenburg Council on Aging from 1990 to 2001. She
  14| served on boards for the Central MA Agency on Aging, the Central MA Association of Councils on Aging, and the Monta-
  15| chusett Opportunity Council and its Nutrition Advisory Board. She also served as a SHINE (Serving Health Insurance
  16| Needs of Elders) counselor at Eagle House. In the early years of Fitchburg Access TV, she hosted Senior Lifestyles. She
  17| was a familiar face at Eagle House where she contributed hundreds of volunteer hours and was a member of the book club.
  18| Jean was instrumental in securing a state grant to expand Eagle House Senior Center in 1998. In 1998 she received a DOVE
  19| Award from the Massachusetts Legislature in recognition of her dedicated volunteer service to elders.
  20| For many years Jean was the Lunenburg correspondent for the Fitchburg Sentinel and the Times Free Press. From 2001 to
  21| 2008 she worked for The Lunenburg Ledger and wrote a popular column called "From the Eye of the Eagle" in which she
  22| voiced her views on town politics and happenings, colored with a bit of local history.
  23|  Lawrence W. Marshall
  24| Larry was born in Leominster and attended Leominster High School and Amherst College. He was active in the Boy Scouts
  25| of America since his youth and continued his compassion for service with his youngest son's Troop 1798 of Lunenburg;
  26| proudly assisting them to achieve the rank of Eagle Scout. He owned various businesses in the area, and was proud to be a
  27| well-respected real estate appraiser for over 40 years. He was first an extremely devoted husband and father and enjoyed
  28| spending time with his family and gardening. Looked on as a mentor, advisor and friend to many, Larry will be sorely
  29| missed and forever remembered and loved.

===PAGE 6=== (plain, 37 lines)
   1| 6
   2| LUNENBURG PROFILE
   3| YEAR INCORPORATED- 1728
   4| REGISTERED VOTERS-7115
   5| FY’16 TAX RATE-$19.62
   6| OPEN TOWN MEETING GOVERNMENT- FIVE MEMBER BOARD OF SELECTMEN
   7| ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- 1ST SATURDAY IN MAY
   8| ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   9| AREA- 26.63 SQUARE MILES
  10| ROAD MILES- ABOUT 100
  11| Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It  occupies a  land area of
  12| approximately 26.4 square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts
  13| approximately 43 miles from Boston.  It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and the
  14| City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on the east
  15| by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of Townsend.
  16| Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting o f all registered voters
  17| in the t own. Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeti ng, the affairs of the t own
  18| are generally administered by a five member Board of S electmen and a  Town Manager. Local
  19| school affairs are administered by an elected seven member School Committee while local taxes
  20| are assessed by a three member Board of A ssessors, all are appointed on an at -large basis for
  21| staggered three-year terms.
  22| Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County.  Three water bodies
  23| account for somewhat of a clustered approach to the t own's historical development.  Lake
  24| Whalom and the former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District from the
  25| remainder of Lunenburg.  This district was developed as a housing base for  many factory
  26| workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster and Fitchburg.   In many ways the
  27| characteristics of the Whalom District more closely reflect characteristics of these cities  than of
  28| the rest of Lunenb urg. The Hickory Hills area of t own, around Hickory Hills Lake, was
  29| developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages.  The third body of
  30| water is Shirley Re servoir, a public water body also developed with m ainly vacation cottages
  31| that have now been modified to year -round residences. Commercial and industrial development
  32| is permitted in several areas of town , primarily along the two major arteries (Rout es 2A and 13),
  33| and the t own's peripheries , due largely to  the close proximity of Leominster's and Fitchburg's
  34| sewer and water systems.  Although many farms and orchards had slowly developed into single
  35| family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg.  At the Annual Town Meeting on May 4 th,
  36| 2013, the Town voted to establish an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve, and
  37| enhance farming in the community and to administer the “Right to Farm” bylaw.

===PAGE 18=== (plain, 38 lines)
   1| 18
   2| REPORT OF THE
   3| TOWN MANAGER
   4| The calendar year of 2016 was a year of progress for the Board of Selectmen and Town Manager’s
   5| Office.  During 2016 there are many accomplishments I would like to highlight:
   6|  In February the Building Reuse Committee presented the results of the Feasibility Study on the
   7| Building Assessment and Space Needs of the town departments performed by Tappe Associates.
   8| The preferred option of the Committee was to demolish parts of TC Passios School and renovate
   9| portions due to the sufficient space and cost. The Board agreed to move forward on this
  10| alternative with the possibility of renting/leasing a portion of that building.  At the Annual Town
  11| Meeting it was voted to transfer the custody and control of the T.C. Passios and Brooks House
  12| from the School Committee to the Board of Selectmen.  Further, at the November 2016 Special
  13| Town Meeting the Building Reuse Committee submitted an article to convey for the purpose of
  14| leasing or selling the Ritter Building, Town Hall, old Primary School, Brooks House, and T.C.
  15| Passios.   This article did not receive a majority vote but the use of these bu ildings continues to
  16| be a topic of interest.
  17|  During the month of March the Board signed a three year Collective Bargaining Agreement with
  18| the Professional Firefighter’s Association and the Board discussed the role and responsibility of
  19| the Selectmen, including being a liaison to various committees. Also in March DPW Director
  20| Jack Rodriquenz presented an updated Pavement Management Plan for roadway improvements
  21| of all town roads that included a proposal for a $4 Million dollar voter authorized debt exclusion
  22| that passed at both a town meeting and ballot vote.  The work will be done over a 4 year period
  23| and would be financed over a period at least twice as long. The Town will attempt to put an
  24| additional $50,000 each fiscal year in the Pavement Management Line Item Account, so there
  25| would be sufficient funding for roadway improvements going forward.
  26|  The Summer Street redevelopment and roadway improvement project continued to move
  27| forward with a slated beginning of 2017.  The total project costs of $10M will be funded through
  28| the Regional Transportation Improvement Plan (TIP), which are state and federal transportation
  29| funds.
  30|  The Town received notice in May that the Tennessee Gas Pipeline Company had suspended any
  31| further work on the Northeast Energy Direct Project, which the Town had declared opposition to
  32| in February of 2015.
  33|  The Annual Town Meeting on May 7th passed a balanced budget for FY 2018 with an omnibus
  34| budget of $33,802,328.  Amendments to the FY 2018 budget were made at both the August and
  35| November Special Town Meetings for a revised total of $34,068,192.
  36|  The Special Town Meeting on August 22nd provided for a surplus of state revenue to be
  37| transferred into the reserve fund and a portion into the school budget. The STM also approved
  38| funding for the DPW Collective Bargaining Agreement and approval of two  rooftop solar

   4|     metering agreement for a project at a non-town owned parcel.  Zoning articles as well as
   5|     a boundary line agreement on Chestnut Street and a 10% match for an APR property for
   6|     Lanni Orchards was on the warrant.
   7|    The Selectmen heard a presentation from World Tech Engineering on the Complete Streets pro-

   9|    The Special Town Meeting on November 28th included amending the FY 2017 budget, creating a

  11|     Salary Administration Plan, and amending the FY 2017 Cable Access budget.
  12|    A Request for Proposals was advertised for Legal Counsel Services with proposals due in De-

  14|    The Selectmen received the resignation of former Town Manager Kerry Lafleur in July and the
  15|     Town began the process of searching for an Interim Town Manager and subsequently a perma-
  16|     nent Town Manager.    Interim Town Manager David Marciello was appointed in September and
  17|     served until December.  The search for a permanent Town Manager began in September with a
  18|     final decision made in December to appoint Heather Lemieux.
  19| At the time of writing this report I have worked for the Town of Lunenburg for just under three
  20| months.  In my short amount of time on the job, it is apparent there is an abundance of wonderful
  21| people that make up the Town of Lunenburg.  Thank you to the Board of Selectmen for the level of
  22| respect that each of you gives your fellow members, the staff, and the public and well thought out
  23| decisions.  Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine Peterson, for all your hard work and keep-
  24| ing the office running smoothly.  I would also like to thank the department heads and staff for the

  30| Director; Kathryn Herrick, Town Clerk;  and all their staff for their talent and working together to
  31| make our town run as efficiently as possible.  Lastly, thank you to the residents and businesses that
  32| make Lunenburg a wonderful place to visit, work, and live.

===PAGE 52=== (layout, 25 lines)
   1|                                              BUILDING DEPARTMENT
   2| The Building Department is located on the second floor of the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts
   3| Avenue, Lunenburg, MA.  Office hours for the Building Department are Monday and Wednesday 8:00 A.M.
   4| to 4:00 P.M., Tuesday and Thursday 8:00 A.M. until 6:00 P.M., closed Friday.  Michael J. Sauvageau serves
   5| as the Building Commissioner, John Morreale serves as the Assistant Building Inspector and Lisa Nor-
   6| mandin serves as the Administrative Assistant.
   7| The Building Department processes all Wiring, Plumbing and Gas permits, this can be done by mail or in
   8| person during regular business hours.
   9| Building permits for 2016 increased slightly from 452 in 2015 to 500 for 2016.  Solar permits remain steady
  10| as well as new single family homes and townhouses. The Emerald Place (formerly Whalom Park) project is
  11| now completed.  Several large projects are in the reviewing stage or have been approved and are anticipated
  12| to begin construction soon. The new middle/high school is now complete and absolutely beautiful.
  13| The 2009 International Building Code for residential and commercial construction is the current building
  14| code and it is anticipated that a new Ninth Edition Code (2015 International Building Code) will soon be in
  15| effect.
  16| The new energy code will be in place on January 1, 2017 based on 2015 International Energy Conservation
  17| Code with the “stretch code” amendments/. Residents and developers are reminded that Lunenburg is a
  18| “Stretch Code Community” which has specific code requirements in addition to the current energy
  19| code requirements.
  20| Residents should be aware that Massachusetts Law requires that all persons, partnerships and corporations
  21| who bid or perform residential contracting (reconstruction, alterations, additions etc.) for work exceeding
  22| $1000.00 be registered with the Commonwealth.  This office will enforce this provision; however you
  23| should ask your contractor about his/her registration.  Please contact this office with any questions about this
  24| requirement or call for any information regarding zoning requirements or building code issues.

   1| 54
   2| CONSERVATION COMMISSION
   3| The Town of Lunenburg Conservation Commission is a seven member commission appointed to 3 year terms by the
   4| Board of Selectmen, whose duties are codified under the Conse rvation Commission Act MGL c. 40 § 8C. The
   5| Commission enforces the Massachusetts Wetland Protection Act MGL c. 131 § 40 and Article XXII of the local
   6| bylaws for the protection of wetlands.
   7| The year 2016 was, as last year, one of a very busy and active agenda containing both public hearings and meetings
   8| for a wide range of projects from commercial development, subdivisions and small single family upkeep projects.
   9| The Conservation Commission worked to spell out goals of preparing to use the ye ar 2017 to complete revising its
  10| regulations to provide consistency with the original bylaw and to find those sections that may be now out of date and
  11| need to be modified. This effort should conclude by the fall of 2017.
  12| The Commission held numerous inspections with the Commission Administrator on weekends prior to meetings to
  13| familiarize themselves with the upcoming proposals that would appear on their agenda. The Commission is also
  14| working with the town on familiarizing itself with the upcoming storm water mandates from the EPA and working
  15| towards compliance both by procedure and with any necessary codificatio n.
  16| The Conservation Commission in cooperation with the Open Space Co mmittee and the Lunenburg Snow Riders
  17| Club has closed out a grant that obtained the former Lane proper ty, known as the Holman Conservation Area, as
  18| open space and has worked to place an adequate and ADA-complaint parking area and bridges that would blend in
  19| with the trail heads on Holman Street. The Conservation Commission also held public recreational walks through its
  20| open space parcels and plans to continue to do so.
  21| The Commission has worked closely with the Lake Shirley Improvement Corporation and Hickory Hills Lake
  22| Management Group on updates of their management plans and becoming actively involved in formulating the new
  23| updates designed to give a better picture of the overall health of the ecosystems and the various ways to ensure
  24| continued protection of these valuable resources.
  25| The Commission also obtained over $16,000 in grant money from DCR for the completion of forest stewardship
  26| planning on the Holman Conservation Area, both Town Forests, the open space on Robbs Hill and the Cowdrey Lot.
  27| The Commission retained Gary Gouldrup, a noted forester with many years of experience working in town for the
  28| commission and private landowners.
  29| This year also marked the hiring of former commission consulting agent Matthew S. Marro as the new Conservation
  30| Administrator. With his 18 years of dealing with commission projects and goals, Mr. Marro was ready to hit the
  31| ground running on day one.  Mr. Marro has worked with the commission this year on office goals and procedures,
  32| increased inspections and office productivity as well as special projects . For example, he publishes a monthly article
  33| in the Lunenburg Ledger addressing the commission goal of informing the public on local environmental and
  34| recreational issues.
  35| The Commission thanks all the town agencies for a great year and look forward to many more!
  36| Respectfully Submitted,
  37| Richard Bursch, Chairman
  38| Todd Dwyer, Vice-Chairman
  39| Tom Bertram
  40| Kenneth Jones
  41| Carl Luck
  42| Bob Pease
  43| Jack Rabbitt
  44| Matthew S. Marro, Conservation Administrator

   2| COUNCIL ON AGING
   3| July 1, 2015 – June 30, 2016
   4| The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
   5| supportive services, programs and education.  Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts
   6| General Law Chapter 40, §8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or
   7| services and to develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues.
   8| The FY16 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative
   9| Assistant and Transportation; Faith A. Anderson, Outreach Coordinator; Joanne Brazell, Meal Site
  10| Coordinator;  Jeanne Tatro, Ruth Swiecicki, Judy Alario and Elsa Watson, Meal Site Assistants; Jerry
  11| Beloin, Jim McGuigan, and Paul Grunditz, MART Van Drivers.
  12| The Council on Aging Board is an eleven member board appointed by the Board of Selectmen. The FY16
  13| Council on Aging Board members were; Chairperson Pete Lincoln,  Vice-Chairperson Jacquelyn Dwyer,
  14| Betty DiGiacomo,  Sarah Grant, Brian Guenard, Deb Lincoln, Kevin McNally, Cheryl Moisan, Diane
  15| Nowd, Jane Rabbitt, and Judith Tarbell.
  16| The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House
  17| Supporters Inc., a non-profit 501(c) 3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of
  18| Massachusetts, and Montachusett Regional Transit Authority.
  19| We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle House.  In FY16 we had 43 volunteers who
  20| contributed 3,149.75 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the Meals on
  21| Wheels Program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, office help, library, and friendly visitors.  We greatly
  22| appreciate their service.
  23| The Council on Aging widened its connection with the community by changing the name of the senior
  24| center to “Eagle House Senior Community Center” and now welcomes all people age 50 or older in
  25| Lunenburg and surrounding communities to participate in our activities.  A new Facebook page was also
  26| created by COA board member Jacquelyn Dwyer.  The Eagle House extended its hours of operation to
  27| include evening programming on Wednesdays.
  28| A $3,000 grant was received from the Nashoba Valley Healthcare Fund with financial support from the
  29| Greater Lowell Community Foundation and the Community Foundation or North Central Massachusetts.
  30| This grant was used to reinstate the “Are You OK?” telephone reassurance program.  This program
  31| provides free daily computerized wellness calls to homebound elders or people with disabilities.  These
  32| calls are generated through the Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District.
  33| The Eagle House offers a variety of programs, such as health and wellness, arts and crafts,
  34| intergenerational, bocce, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, and social events both at the
  35| senior center and offsite.  There were 778 people who utilized the senior center in FY16.  Those 778
  36| people compiled 21,409 units of service by participating in the programs listed above.

   1| 59
   2| DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   3| The Department of Public Works combines the resources of the Highway, Park, Cemetery, Sewer, and
   4| Facilities divisions, and Vehicle Maintenance services into one department organizing and scheduling personnel
   5| and equipment to best serve the residents of the community through the effective delivery of an extensive scope
   6| of services with a high degree of efficiency.
   7| DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
   8|  “Public Works is either working or on call twenty four hours a day three hundred and sixty five days a
   9| year to respond to any weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility related emergency that may arise.”
  10| We strive to enhance the awareness of the value of Public Works provides to our Town, and ensure oth-
  11| ers are aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to make sure efforts are not
  12| forgotten as they merge into the fabric of our everyday service.
  13| Major events over the past years have highlighted the fact that Public Works has a seat at the table and is
  14| a major player in emergency management. Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is
  15| critical in order to facilitate the required services of others. We must continue to build on this awareness.
  16| This department must also reach out to the public we serve and educate them to the fact that the common
  17| services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated staff of individuals, service
  18| companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control, solid waste collection and recycling operations,
  19| tree removal, storm water management facilities and grounds maintenance, winter snow and ice removal, ceme-
  20| teries and parks are the expected services supporting everyone in the community.
  21| We will work to engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather support for annual
  22| budget requests, capital requests, and specific projects.
  23| PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  24| The updated Pavement Management Plan, inclusive of an electronic database and a revised Town -wide
  25| pavement defect analysis. For a second time, a complete survey of every road was completed to establish a
  26| “pavement defect value.” The amount of new paving does not keep up with the yearly deterioration factor, and
  27| as such the backlog is in excess of nine million dollars. The program remains underfunded however efforts of the
  28| Town Manager and the Board of Selectmen have resulted in a separate line within the DPW budget earmarked
  29| solely for paving and paving related services.
  30| Road Projects typically extend over two construction seasons and overlap from one fiscal year to the
  31| next. During this year the paving schedule included: a large portion of Howard and West St., West Townsend
  32| Rd., Northfield and Flat Hill Rd. each funded through the Chapter 90 State Aid program with the exclusion of
  33| Flat Hill Rd. completed through the operational budget of the DPW by a dedicated Pavement Management Plan
  34| line. Due to a hellacious winter of 2014 -2015, the Governor allocated a sum of money to each city/Town in the
  35| Commonwealth under the description of “Winter Rapid Recovery Program” to be used exclusively for paving.
  36| The completion of Howard St., a portion of Reservoir Rd., Arbor St., Page St., Kilburn St., and West St. all ben-
  37| efitted from the program with a curb to curb overlay along the most degraded portions.
  38| Documentation provided through a “pothole” log (a daily record of road work specific to patching and
  39| filling of pot holes) provided information that one third of all the available work days during the season are dedi-
  40| cated to patching and other temporary repairs to similar roadway failures. As defects are identified we respond to
  41| every location in a timely manner. This has proven to be as less than effective method due to the fact that patch-
  42| ing is merely a temporary fix. New pot holes appear each spring particularly after a severe winter, and often staff
  43| addresses the same areas year after year and must continue to do so until full curb to curb rehabilitation is com-
  44| pleted, clearly a revenue driven operation.
  45| The seasonal crack sealing program has been effective in some locations however this application is de-
  46| signed as a temporary solution as well with an effective life span of around five years. Many streets that have
  47| been “sealed” are well beyond that time frame. The mechanic of the process involves the installation of rubber-
  48| ized asphalt under high pressure into cracks or separations in the pavement. The Pavement Management Plan
  49| identifies specific areas where this method is the best option based upon the aforementioned pavement defect
  50| value  This application does not take the place of curb to curb paving, only postpones it and is not applicable in
  51| areas of more serious defects.

===PAGE 60=== (layout, 48 lines)
   1|           The revised Pavement plan, no matter how detailed, is of little value without an identified source of
   2| funding. Lack of revenue continues to be the major detriment to improving the overall condition of our road-
   3| ways. The primary sources of funding are the Massachusetts Department of Transportation Chapter 90 pro-
   4| gram and the dedicated line in the DPW budget.  The estimated value of our 86 miles of paved streets is, by
   5| industry standards, calculated to be 62 million dollars. The General Highway maintenance line within the
   6| operational budget has remained virtually unchanged for two decades while the overall omnibus budget has
   7| nearly tripled to a value in excess of 30 million dollars. In order to initiate and sustain a road improvement
   8| program a substantial infusion of funds is necessary.   The conservative estimate remains at approximately
   9| 1.2 million dollars per year total or an additional $800,000.00 to existing allocations. Applying $400,000-
  10| $500,000 a year to a backlog of work that approaches 10 million dollars dooms the current plan to failure.
  11| As an example: many roads are compromised to the degree that only a major reconstruction can bring them
  12| to an acceptable quality and less travelled, local roads remain out of reach.
  13| FLEET MAINTENANCE
  14|           The DPW is responsible for the repairs and preventative maintenance to all Town vehicles and
  15| equipment. High performance police units, First responder Fire and Rescue apparatus, park, cemetery, and
  16| school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway related machinery are serviced by department person-
  17| nel. Outsourcing of highly technical repairs has become more frequent and therefore more costly. Due to the
  18| volatile global oil market, the overall cost of fuel and petroleum based products continues to be a moving
  19| target and therefore difficult to estimate annually.
  20|           The installation of a vehicle maintenance software program has proven to be a valuable tool. Each
  21| repair is logged by department and vehicle identification number and memorialized in a data bank. Every
  22| vehicle can be tracked for part numbers, required services, as well as frequency and cost of repairs over the
  23| life of the equipment. As Director I have created and submitted to Town Officials a vehicle replacement
  24| schedule created by the American Public Works Association in order to more clearly represent our needs.
  25| FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
  26| The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:
  27|                DPW Garage 520 Chase Road                              Public Safety Complex
  28|                Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.                                 Town Hall
  29|                Cemetery Building, Holman St.                          Eagle House
  30|                Town Beach                                             Town Common
  31|                Ritter Bldg.                                              Teen Center
  32|                Old Primary School                                         Veterans Park
  33|                All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding school properties
  34|      The ever increasing workload made necessary the addition of a second individual to assist with repairs
  35| and maintenance. The increase in staff by only one has significantly lessened the need for outsourcing, spe-
  36| cifically preventative maintenance. Staff remains on-call 24 hours a day 365 days per year. Staff keeps
  37| buildings compliant and protects the safe running of the work environment.
  38|       Any significant interruption of service within Town buildings carries the potential of disrupting or disa-
  39| bling necessary services provided to the community. The facilities support staff are problem solvers and
  40| technicians with a wide range of experience encompassing all of the construction trades. They deliver ser-
  41| vices or oversee those functions provided by specialized subcontractors. Staff manages and forecasts all of
  42| the building assets and prioritizes replacement costs. Unfortunately this component of the program has fallen
  43| short of expectations and need and with the formation of a Building Reuse Committee it is clear that asset
  44| management relative to our buildings requires a professionally prepared capital plan.  With the use of build-
  45| ing maintenance software, requests for service are acknowledged immediately, prioritized, and completed in
  46| a timely manner. Those individuals requesting service are contacted upon completion of the tasks a history
  47| of the repair is filed electronically. Although a success, this remains a reactionary approach.
  48|                                                                  60

===PAGE 61=== (layout, 50 lines)
   1| ENVIRONMENTAL
   2| Storm Water
   3|          For yet another year the Environmental Protection Agency has not set the requirements for the next
   4| phase of storm water regulation and permits. As we await the potential of what will be an unfunded Federal
   5| mandate the Town remains in compliance with current protocol.
   6|          The DPW, as a result of limited funding is forced to become reactionary to roadway drainage issues. A
   7| capital program in the 1990’s earmarked $20,000.00 annually toward rehabilitation/installation of piping and
   8| catch basins. The elimination of this dedicated funding placed the financial burden upon the overall General
   9| Highway maintenance allocation effectively reducing drainage repairs. In anticipation of the new regulations
  10| the Town Manager has re-established a budget line within the DPW dedicated to storm water, a necessary first
  11| step.
  12|          As roadways are scheduled for pavement, drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are addressed to
  13| avoid paving over problems. New storm water regulations will likely force the Town to become more proac-
  14| tive albeit with no identified source of funding. At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall drainage
  15| structures in close proximity to open water are cleaned .Public Works attempts to maintain an aggressive
  16| schedule of street sweeping and catch basin cleaning, the front line of storm water management.
  17| Waste Water
  18|          The DPW continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system. To become more efficient the Sewer
  19| Commission has outsourced much of the day to day inspections and preventative maintenance of the overall
  20| wastewater system inclusive of 10 pump stations.  Business management, inspections, permitting, and over-
  21| sight of the subcontractor remain the responsibility of the DPW.
  22| Tree Removal
  23|          While it may seem difficult to believe, the infamous “Ice Storm of ‘08” continues to detrimentally af-
  24| fect trees in the Town right-of-way. As presented to the Board of Selectman during the initial recovery pro-
  25| cess, the “heartwood” of a large volume of trees was affected. Previously unnoticed damage together with nat-
  26| ural attrition of every species creates a backlog of tree felling and pruning that far exceeds the allotted budget.
  27| The department is unable to fulfill each resident request and must prioritize work based upon the calculated
  28| degree of danger to residents and property. With the establishment of a Hazardous Tree Survey, a more effi-
  29| cient approach to defects has been established by prioritization. Sadly, the amount of work far exceeds the
  30| available revenue. Until a plan is in place, the DPW will act in a reactionary mode. All work aside from brush
  31| removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees is outsourced due to the necessity of a bucket truck
  32| and a licensed professional. Each spring DPW staff initiates the activity of removing and disposing of woody
  33| debris within the right-of-way and a Town-wide program of curbside mowing. A hugely aggressive program
  34| by our power provider has lessened the burden of the Town to remove those trees that may cause an interrup-
  35| tion of electrical service to the community.
  36| Landfill
  37|          The “capped” landfill off Young’s Rd. remains in compliance with the inspection protocol mandated
  38| by the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive methane gas as well
  39| as the possibility of groundwater contamination.
  40|          The frequency of the testing was reduced through a special permit acquired as a result of consistently
  41| favorable results. The landfill site including the perimeter fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW
  42| personnel.
  43|          Adjacent to the “cap” is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined
  44| by the EPA to be hazardous waste. We are permitted to store these materials through the procurement of a
  45| (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires yearly testing of these materials. Other com-
  46| munities are required to transport these tainted materials to an appropriate site at an excessive cost.
  47|          On weekends during the fall and springtime months DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual
  48| composting of brush, leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and add a
  49| valuable component to our recycling program. There has to date been no dedicated funding toward
  50|                                                                   61

===PAGE 62=== (plain, 49 lines)
   1| 62
   2| the organization or long range remediation of this location. Once again, dollars will be taken from
   3| the General Highway line to maintain this location while keeping it environmentally safe. It is likely this de-
   4| partment will present a plan to use the compost as leverage to clean and grade the area as well as the removal
   5| of the brush. With water and sewer at the perimeter this sandy, gravelly area adjacent has been proposed as a
   6| potential recreational are for the Town. As of yet this project has not gained traction.
   7| Solid Waste
   8| Public works monitors, orders, stores, and distributes trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside
   9| collection of trash and recyclables for our by our current vendor, Casella Inc for the pay as you throw pro-
  10| gram.
  11| Lake Shirley Dam
  12|              In the early 1990’s, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding the Lake Shirley Association
  13| turned over ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for
  14| maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed solely by a small number of volunteers from
  15| the Lake Association. In 2004 -2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the
  16| establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the umbrella of
  17| the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates compli-
  18| ance with current and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a professional
  19| consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection resulted in a positive report.
  20| Winter Operations
  21| Snow and ice present a major challenge to our Town. The safety of the general public depends on
  22| the ability of the DPW to maintain our roadways in a safe condition despite the severity or the duration of
  23| snow and ice events. To that end we have developed a winter operations procedure based upon a detailed
  24| plan that must be flexible due to the unpredictability of each storm. A consistently high level of service is
  25| expected and must be delivered to every part of Town in a timely manner.
  26| Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the capital plan.  Financial
  27| support is necessary to provide effective fleet rotation insuring minimal interruption of services due to break-
  28| downs and repairs.  In the fall, salt and sand are delivered to the Chase Rd. facility. Lunenburg is in a consor-
  29| tium with 14 other communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible price for road salt an absolute ne-
  30| cessity. Individually the Town also solicits bids for road sand. From late summer to early fall vehicle mainte-
  31| nance staff inspects and prepares the trucks, sanders and plows for winter operations. During the construc-
  32| tion season road crews address right -of-way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects in order to
  33| eliminate obstacles to plowing. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter operations. Typically
  34| DPW staff combines with 18-20 subcontractors to provide service.
  35| Generally it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event that determines the overall cost.
  36| For example, a fast moving snow storm of 6 -8 inches will cost significantly less than a long duration freez-
  37| ing rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically, the department operates in a deficit
  38| due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average winter. It is a dedicated group of individuals
  39| who successfully challenge the worst Mother Nature has to offer regardless of severity or duration.
  40| “DPW workers are the original Storm Troopers”
  41| PARKS
  42| In conjunction with the three elected Park Commissioners DPW staff oversees and maintains all
  43| parks and grounds exclusive of school property. A full time Park employee was replaced with seasonal
  44| workers. Much of the Town wide landscape maintenance is outsourced, resulting in significant savings and
  45| increased efficiency.  Repair of the multi-use track complex is in the planning stage. After many years, a mu-
  46| tual effort between the Commissioners and all users of the facility is gaining traction.
  47| CEMETERY
  48| One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and South
  49| cemeteries, maintaining the grounds, coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three

===PAGE 63=== (plain, 10 lines)
   1| 63
   2| members of the Cemetery Commission.  The DPW supplies equipment and support staff for, on aver-
   3| age, forty burials a year and general maintenance of the facilities.
   4| As always, I am deeply appreciative of the continued patience and support of our residents and fellow
   5| Town employees as we persevere.
   6| “As caretakers of the Town’s infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the
   7| Town of Lunenburg for future generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associat-
   8| ed with the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella of public works.”
   9| Jack Rodriquenz
  10| Director

===PAGE 64=== (layout, 42 lines)
   1|                                                 FINANCE COMMITTEE
   2|        (This is a reprint of the message printed in the May 7, 2016 Annual Town Meeting warrant)
   3| The Town Budget to be presented at Town Meeting has followed the same process as in recent years.  The
   4| Town Manager has again provided Department Heads a ‘target budget’ to work from, based on the financial
   5| forecast, current service levels and funding opportunities.
   6| The Finance Committee is recommending approval of the Town budget in the amount of $33,802,328.00,
   7| with an increase in revenues from all sources, totaling $1,758,413 over the 2016 Budget (after recap).  A sig-
   8| nificant increase is anticipated in State Aid from Chapter 70 (education funding) and general government aid
   9| (lottery).    While this is welcome news, unrestricted general government aid has yet to reach its previous
  10| 2006 levels.  Furthermore, since the financial forecast was first presented, health insurance costs have come
  11| in much greater than expected.  For the most part, Town departments have continued to maintain a consistent
  12| portion of the budget, but pressures from a few drivers, namely Retirement, Health Insurance and Debt Ser-
  13| vice, are beginning to demand a larger share, creating constraints on growth.
  14| In breaking down the revenue to the Town, 67% comes from real estate property taxes and 33% comes from
  15| other sources such as State Aid, motor vehicle excise or meal taxes, betterments and other fees.  The effects
  16| of such aid on particular aspects of the budget are difficult to parse out, except for Chapter 70 funding, which
  17| accounts for $1 of every $3 budgeted for the schools.
  18| Retirement and health insurance consume an ever increasing portion of the total budget.   Town employees
  19| pay into 1 of 2 retirement systems.  Licensed school district employees contribute to the Massachusetts
  20| Teacher Retirement System, which requires no additional town funding.  Any additional funding of this Re-
  21| tirement System is done at the State level, and is reported in notes to the Town Financial Statements as an
  22| ‘On Behalf’ payment.  Non-licensed school personnel and Town-side employees contribute to the Worcester
  23| County Retirement System.  The Town makes two payments to this system, an employer contribution for
  24| active employees and an assessment to fund the ‘Unfunded Liability’ of future payments to retirees.  The
  25| Retirement Assessment has gone up 8.7%, or $84,143, over the 2016 Budget (after recap). Health insurance
  26| represents a 9.1% increase in spending and a 0.3% increase in budget share. The Town has been able to ne-
  27| gotiate changes to health plans over the past few years to help reduce cost, moving to a 75% Town, 25% em-
  28| ployee cost sharing. This year premiums are projected to increase by 12.8%, which affects both the Town
  29| budget, and the School Department budget.
  30| Even if the funding was available to do so, the Finance Committee and Town Administration have expressed
  31| reluctance to add new positions to departments until the issue of future liability to the Town for both retire-
  32| ment and other post-employment health benefits (OPEB) are adequately addressed.  In the meantime, where
  33| it is possible to consolidate current positions to better meet the needs of the Town, such changes have been
  34| proposed.  For example, consolidating a part time administrative position and a contracted consultant into
  35| one full-time position in the Conservation Commission office and adding hours to existing positions in Infor-
  36| mation Technology department and in the Assessors’ office to meet increased workloads.
  37| Debt Service has 14% budget share, up a quarter percent with a 6.6%, or $298,911 increase, the main reason
  38| being the new Middle/High School, as well as existing debt from the Primary School, Public Safety Build-
  39| ing, and Library.  We are still several years out from seeing these projects paid down.  The Capital Planning
  40| Committee has recommended funding 12 items at $780,975; this budget funds 10 items at $619,475.   There
  41| is no request or recommendation to borrow money to fund the remaining items.
  42|                                                                  64

===PAGE 65=== (layout, 24 lines)
   1| There is however a warrant article to see if the Town is willing to fund the Pavement Management Plan
   2| through a debt exclusion.  This will not impact the budget in FY17 but if approved would increase the debt
   3| service starting in FY18.  The Finance Committee will make its recommendation on this article at Town
   4| Meeting.
   5| Because Retirement, Health Insurance and Debt Service are taking up more of the budget, general spending
   6| for the School Department, Public Safety, and the DPW are seeing decreases in budget share.
   7| The Police and Fire Chiefs continue to express a need for additional staffing to meet an ever more demand-
   8| ing work load, but the requests have not been accommodated in this budget.  Regional Dispatch will be add-
   9| ing another community which will reduce Lunenburg’s assessment and free up $7,000 to increase weekend
  10| coverage at the Public Safety Building.
  11| The School Committee budget request is only $21,072 greater than the amount in the budget presented here,
  12| which assumes the Governor’s recommended budget for Chapter 70 funding makes it through the final ver-
  13| sion of the Commonwealth’s budget.  The additional Chapter 70 funding reduced the target gap by $291,523.
  14| In summary, the budget presented addresses at least current service levels with little room to discuss priori-
  15| ties beyond nondiscretionary items.  The Finance Committee will continue to assure the budget addresses the
  16| priorities of the Town of Lunenburg as we understand them.  We congratulate the Town administration’s
  17| work to maintain the AA+ bond rating which allows the Town to borrow at competitive rates, saving taxpay-
  18| ers significant funds over time.  We thank all of the town employees for their continued service, and are
  19| grateful for the continued cooperation of the Town Manager, Finance Director, and each department head to
  20| ensure that every question is answered.

   1|                                          INFORMATION TECHNOLOGY
   2| The primary mission of the Information Technology Department is to increase efficiency through the use of
   3| technology.  The IT Department enables all other town departments and schools to communicate and collab-
   4| orate, manage daily tasks, organize, store, and share information, and generally conduct Town business
   5| more effectively.  We also provide the necessary infrastructure, equipment, software, and expertise to com-
   6| ply with current regulations and methodology when interacting with outside agencies and organizations.
   7| The Information Technology staff for the Town has remained constant this year, but we have added a new
   8| full time IT position for the Schools.  Following is an overview of our current IT staff:
   9| Steve Malandrinos – Information Technology Director
  10|     Hired in December, 2012
  11| Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA and Belchertown
  12| Public Schools
  13| Daniel Nadareski – Network Administrator
  14|     Retired police officer from the Albany, NY police force with over 24 years on the job
  15| Prior experience includes computer crime and forensics, network administration, video security system ad-
  16| ministration, and IP phone system administration
  17| Jordan Pochini – Tech Support Specialist
  18|     Recently graduated from Fitchburg State University with a degree in Computer Science
  19| Prior experience includes both individual contributor and management roles in retail environments
  20| Nicholas Scoggins – School Technology Coordinator
  21|     Received Associate and Bachelor of Science degrees from ITT Technical Institute
  22| Served 5 years as Security System Administrator at Greater Lawrence Technical School
  23| Along with serving as helpdesk for all users’ daily technology issues and the usual maintenance and sched-
  24| uled replacement of the town’s and schools’ technology assets, the IT Department has been involved with
  25| several high impact projects throughout the past fiscal year.  The following examples are among the more
  26| noteworthy improvements that IT has implemented.
  27| An entirely new backup and disaster recovery solution was implemented at Town Hall, the DPW, and the
  28| Ritter Memorial Building.  Backups are a low visibility, high impact area of IT.  They’re never needed until
  29| they are, and then they are absolutely critical to the stability of the organization.
  30| A large portion of the Technology department’s time this past year was dedicated to the transition from the
  31| previous Lunenburg High School to the new Lunenburg Middle-High School.  Much of our critical infra-
  32| structure was centralized at the old building, requiring detailed planning and work over many nights and
  33| weekends to maintain the services on which our school faculty and staff depend to do their jobs.  We also
  34| spent much of the summer building, configuring, and bringing online the many new systems that are cur-
  35| rently serving our students, faculty and staff.
  36| We continue to strive to leverage limited resources to improve efficiency wherever possible.   I am very
  37| happy to continue in this role, and I could not be more pleased with the level of skill, hard work and dedica-
  38| tion of my staff, Daniel and Jordan.  I know that because of them we will continue to improve the technolo-
  39| gy landscape in Lunenburg while maintaining the positive reputation we have strived for over the past sev-
  40| eral years.
  41|                                  Steve Malandrinos, IT Director

   2| LUNENBURG PUBLIC LIBRARY
   3| The library has had a really good year!  In fact I think the Lunenburg library is just AWESOME!!!  During
   4| the year we welcomed Heather Buiwit as our new Digital Services Librarian.  Heather handles all things E (e
   5| -books, -e-reference tools, e-audio, e-book training, etc.) as well as assisting me with the library’s technolo-
   6| gy (Wi-Fi, trouble shooting computer issues, documenting technical practices).  Heather is also helping out
   7| with marketing the library. She recently created a new Facebook page for us and is the moderator and con-
   8| tent provider for the page.  Jocelyn Lacroix, our new Library Page arrived last fall and has acclimated quite
   9| well.
  10| The Board of Trustees has remained the same this past year although changes are due with this next election
  11| cycle.  This year the Trustees have focused heavily on updating our library policies and a few building pro-
  12| jects. I wish to thank them personally for all their support and guidance.
  13| The library building has had a relatively quiet year. Our new burner has given us wonderful even AND con-
  14| sistent heat throughout the building.  The new building management system has enabled the DPW to get
  15| ahead of any critical events – as it notifies them when it something isn’t working properly.  This year we had
  16| the two quiet study rooms and the story craft room painted and they look great.  Our thanks to the Friends
  17| organization for funding the painting projects.   The Green Community Task Force accepted our grant pro-
  18| posal for the migration of interior lights for the library and the project should be completed by the end of
  19| March 2017. We are hoping this gives us better and less costly lighting.  The library invested in new tables
  20| for the Community Hall and the Story Craft Room.  We also added additional security cameras.
  21| The library has increased the number of programs we provide including a series of exhibits of local artists’
  22| work and a monthly series called Friday Food & Flicks where we provide the movie and attendees bring
  23| their favorite recipes based on a specific theme like soup, casseroles, appetizers and desserts.  We have add-
  24| ed a new and enlarged continuous book sale so people can come and browse a large selection of adult fiction
  25| and non-fiction to purchase with the proceeds going to the Friends group. We will of course continue with
  26| the Friends much loved June & October book & bake sales.
  27| The library has been focused heavily on improving our collections over the last year. In the main library we
  28| have added (and will continue to add) e-books that can only be borrowed by holders of a Lunenburg library
  29| card so you can borrow that bestseller a bit quicker than before.   We now subscribe to digital versions of all
  30| the local newspapers that residents can access online (see our website). We began a very large project of
  31| overhauling the entire nonfiction collection.  Hopefully this will be completed by this summer.
  32| The library is becoming the community center it was meant to be.  We are partnering with many local organ-
  33| izations and providing a venue for them to connect with Lunenburg residents including Community Health
  34| Connections, Lunenburg Historical Society, Boy Scouts, GiftMart, Freedom’s Way, Eagle House/Senior
  35| Center and many more.
  36| FROM THE CHILDREN’S ROOM
  37| The Children’s Room is just bursting with all types of activities.  We have reached out and are partnering
  38| with three different schools to provide story times for children once a week. The Children’s Room now hosts
  39| a series called STEM Saturdays; the program has activities to nurture an interest in science, technology engi-
  40| neering, and math.  Our LEGO programs continue to challenge boys and girls alike.  The Children’s Room
  41| began selling “book bundles” – 3 books on the same theme (each bundle is $1) and it has become a great hit.
  42| The money goes to the Friends organization which enables us to buy special things like a new dollhouse and
  43| a new CD player.

   2| The Children’s Room has continued its project of renewing and expanding the collection.  We have added a

   4| for learning by providing a viable, up-to-date collection of books and media.  These items will inform chil-

  15| games using today’s technology as well as the time-tested models such as a parent, a cozy chair, and a great
  16| book!
  17| FROM THE TEEN ROOM:
  18| In 2015 we began examining the young adult collection, focusing on the manga section.  We continued this
  19| effort in 2016, focusing on the graphic novels, non-fiction, and classics sections.   Each section was reviewed
  20| to make sure that titles were in good shape, contain accurate information, and have strong teen appeal.  In an
  21| effort to make browsing easier, the biographies were separated out of the non-fiction section and made into
  22| their own collection.  A freestanding spinning rack was also introduced.  The spinner houses the newly creat-
  23| ed romance, inspirational fiction, and teen CD collections.
  24| One of our primary goals is to provide access to materials in as many varied formats as possible.  To that ef-
  25| fect, much to the delight of the teens, anime DVDs were introduced.  While this is still a fledgling collection,
  26| it fills a much remarked up gap in the collection and has been met with a very positive response.
  27| The gaming center was expanded to include not just video games but board games as well.  The board game
  28| collection targets all ages not just teens.  It houses two player games, family friendly games, and party games
  29| in a mix of classic and contemporary styles.  A monthly teen board game night was created to introduce teens
  30| to new games and allow fellow gamers a chance to connect.
  31| We will continue to update the teen room, introducing new materials, programs, and technology as we strive
  32| to make the teen room the best, most responsive space it can be.

   1|                           SENIOR PROPERTY TAX WORK-OFF PROGRAM
   2|                                                           Est. 1998
   3|                     CALENDAR YEAR JANUARY 1, 2016 – NOVEMBER 1, 2016
   4| The Property Tax Work-Off Committee is a 5 member committee appointed by the Board of Selectmen.
   5| The Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60+ an oppor-
   6| tunity to receive up to $1,000 off of their property tax bill in the calendar year rather than the fiscal year.
   7| Participants worked in various town departments to earn the tax abatement doing general office duties, cus-
   8| todial, library shelving, and kitchen assistance.
   9| For each hour worked a person received $10 and worked up to 100 hours between January 1, 2016 and No-
  10| vember 1, 2016 to receive the abatement.  Any hours worked beyond that were considered volunteer hours.
  11| There were eleven Lunenburg residents who participated between January 1, 2016 and November 1, 2016.
  12| These individuals, as a whole, accumulated 837.25 hours towards their abatements.
  13| Eligibility Requirements:
  14|    Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  15|    Own and occupy a home in Lunenburg as their primary residence for at least one year
  16|    Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse’s name who is also 60 years of age or older
  17|    Meet the income guidelines of : One person household - $40,000 / Two person household - $60,000
  18|    All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check and must re- apply and be accepted
  19|     annually for participation in the program.
  20| Earnings are exempt from state taxes only.  Earnings are subject to Federal and FICA tax withholdings and
  21| are also subject to OBRA (Omnibus Budget Reconciliation Act of 1990, otherwise known as MA Deferred
  22| Compensation), if participants are not receiving a pension from either Worcester Regional Retirement or
  23| Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement.

===PAGE 76=== (layout, 48 lines)
   1|                                                   SEWER COMMISSION
   2| The five member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6, 2006.
   3| At the first Sewer Commission meeting after the May 2015 Town election (5/26/15) when the Commission
   4| elects the officers for  the new fiscal year, Carl Luck was elected to remain as Chairman, Mike Nault to serve
   5| as Vice-Chair and Troy Daniels to serve as Clerk. Sue Bunish was elected to the Commission by ballot in the
   6| May 2015 election, however neither Dave MacDonald nor Butch Bilotta sought re-election, leaving one vacan-
   7| cy on the 5-member Commission.  On July 7, 2015 John Reynolds was appointed to the Commission to serve a
   8| one-year term by a joint meeting of the Board of Selectmen and the Sewer Commission. The Sewer Commis-
   9| sion bears the responsibility of overseeing the Town’s sewer system, planning for the future, and developing
  10| and implementing rules, regulations and policies.  The Department of Public Works manages and maintains the
  11| operational side of the system.  Under the Town’s Charter, responsibility for the day-to day operation, care and
  12| maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the supervision of the Town Manager.   The Town of Lunenburg
  13| does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 83% of Lunenburg sewer is discharged to the City of
  14| Leominster and the other 17% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg.
  15| There are currently 846 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 393 that have access to
  16| Town Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY16, 37 new accounts connected to Town Sewer.  The Com-
  17| mission billed out $734,145 in quarterly sewer bills, and had $63,086.97 of prior year delinquencies added to
  18| the owners’ property tax bills for non-payment.   Three properties with access to the original sewer installation
  19| were assessed privilege fees that had not originally been assessed betterments.  Privilege fees help to pay down
  20| the debt owed by the Town on the original sewer project.
  21| At the May 2015 Town meeting the Commission had gone before the townspeople to request an additional ap-
  22| propriation of $300,000 for the addition of Pleasant View Ave into the SRF project. It was approved and in
  23| June 2015, the engineers at Wright Pierce began the engineering and design to install sewers in the Pleasant
  24| View Ave. in concert with the SRF project, providing great cost saving by having the same construction firm,
  25| CJP & Sons Construction, complete the installation while their equipment was still in town.  On July 15, 2015
  26| construction to install sewers for the SRF Sewer Project began in the Pratt Street and Lakeview Avenue areas.
  27| Work on the sewer installation continued throughout FY16. In the fall of 2015, Wright Pierce again assisted the
  28| Town by helping the Town obtain a DEP grant of $30,000 for the inspection and evaluation of over 300 man-
  29| holes to determine causes of Inflow/Infiltration (I/I). Wright Pierce began the project in February 2016 and
  30| completed the inspections and analysis in the spring 2016.  The Commission will use their evaluation and rec-
  31| ommendations to alleviate Inflow/Infiltration going into FY17.
  32| In February 2016, the Commission approved revisions of the Sewer Extension Regulation, thanks in large part
  33| to the hard work of Troy Daniels.  The Commission was grateful to Troy in his work to help protect the Town
  34| by putting forward a complete and comprehensive Regulation.  The Commission continued to work hard on the
  35| Grease Trap Regulation to make it a workable document that can be easily followed by all Food Service Estab-
  36| lishments (FSE) that have internal or external grease interceptors. The Commission held two public hearings to
  37| get input for those restaurants and well as the townspeople.  As of May 2016, the regulation was approved, and
  38| a new fee structure and requirements for annual permits, compliance and reporting was instituted.   Lunenburg
  39| has 18 food service establishments with grease traps and interceptors.  This regulation will continue to be
  40| strongly enforced as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance
  41| costs of the sewer system.  While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public disposal, indi-
  42| vidual home owners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which increases every-
  43| one’s costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.
  44| In August 2013, the Sewer Commission began contracting with Small Water Systems Services of Littleton
  45| (SWSS) for the Operations and Management of the 10 pumping stations and sewer infrastructure. The Sewer
  46| Commission has been very satisfied with the personnel, the work and the arrangement with SWSS, renewing
  47| the contract for two successive years.
  48|                                                                    76

===PAGE 77=== (layout, 37 lines)
   1| In April 2016, anticipating the end of the three-year renewable contract, the Sewer Commission put the
   2| maintenance of the sewer system out to bid.  Small Water was awarded the bid and we are happy to contin-
   3| ue this great partnership for 3 more years.  The new contract, effective 7/1/16, was expanded to include the
   4| generators in each pump station and quarterly meter readings for manual and irrigation accounts.
   5| At the May 2016 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward 5 articles, 3 of which related to the budget
   6| and stabilization funds. For the standard budget article to appropriate funds for FY17, $69,775.27 was trans-
   7| ferred from Retained Earnings to cover both general expenses and debt service on the betterments.  The goal
   8| of the Sewer Commission is to maintain a balanced budget going forward and to build up the Retained
   9| Earnings so that it may be used for major capital projects to maintain and improve the operating efficiency
  10| of the system.   The fourth article updated the Sewer Bylaw to mandate the holding of 2 public hearings to
  11| ensure public participation in regulation changes.  The fifth article was to present the updated Comprehen-
  12| sive Wastewater Management Plan (CWMP) for acceptance.  The CWMP needed updating since the origi-
  13| nal master plan was put into place in 2010.  Wright Pierce had completed the original evaluation and plan
  14| and worked with the Commission to update the CWMP.  This master plan is a great source to the Town for
  15| checking compliance and future planning.
  16| The Commission would like to thank Troy Daniels, who resigned from the Commission January 14, 2016
  17| and  Mike Nault, who did not seek re-election in May 2016; also Sue Bunish who resigned from the Sewer
  18| Commission in July 2016 citing personal reasons, for all their hard work and dedication.  In the May 2016
  19| town election John Reynolds was elected to a 3 year term. With the resulting vacancies, two openings were
  20| filled by appointment in July 2016 when the Commission welcomed Joseph Anderson and Joan Pease.
  21| Richard St. Cyr was appointed in October 2016 and we thank them all for their willingness to serve on the
  22| Commission.  Bob Ebersole continues to serve as the Board of Selectmen liaison and the Commission is
  23| grateful for his knowledge and counsel.  Jay Simeone, a past member of the Sewer Commission, serves as
  24| the Finance Committee liaison to blend his experience on the Commission with its financial planning and
  25| we thank him for his participation. The Commission would also like to thank Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Direc-
  26| tor, for the seamless and smooth operation of the Sewer Division of the DPW and Jim Breault (DPW-
  27| Facilities) who whenever called upon, provides his expertise and service to the Sewer Division.  They
  28| would also like to acknowledge and thank the Lunenburg Water District for their partnership and good
  29| working relationship with the DPW and especially with Business Manager, Barb Lefebvre, in providing
  30| valuable assistance with meter readings, ownership changes, and status updates.

  27| who want to have their property sprayed can do so by emailing to:  cmmcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-

  29| office.

  31| illness and Hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  32| Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.

===PAGE 85=== (plain, 46 lines)
   1| 85
   2| TOWN OF LUNENBURG, MASSACHUSETTS
   3| FIRE DEPARTMENT
   4| The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are
   5| dedicated to providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to
   6| the citizens of the Town of Lunenburg.
   7| In 2016 your Fire Department responded to 1986 calls for service, broken down as follows:
   8| Fires & Hazardous Conditions                 475                Emergency Medical      965
   9| Inspections                  508                  Other                             38
  10| Fire Department staffing today is five Career and forty-forty-five On Call Firefighters and
  11| Emergency Medical Technicians.  The station is manned seven days per week, but only from 6:00 a.m. -
  12| 6:00 p.m.  During those hours the station is manned with two-three Firefighter/Emergency Medical
  13| Technicians, plus the Chief of Department and EMS Coordinator weekdays.  This coverage is a
  14| combination of full-time personnel in conjunction with 72 hours per week of per diem coverage.  The per
  15| diem coverage is provided by our Call Firefighter/EMT’s manning the station at their hourly call rate.
  16| At night however, the station is unmanned and all of our personnel respond from home on an “on
  17| call” basis.   This adds several minutes or more to our response time as personnel must respond to the
  18| station, pick up the appropriate equipment and then respond the call.
  19| It should be noted that except for the addition of forty-eight hours of per diem coverage on
  20| Saturday and Sunday (2 per diem Firefighter/EMTs from 6:00 a.m. - 6:00 p.m. each day), our staffing
  21| model has remained unchanged for over fifteen years.  In this same time period we have seen an almost
  22| 48% increase in the call volume that we respond to.
  23| Our call volume in 2016 matched what we experienced in 2015, which was our busiest year to
  24| date.  As our community continues to grow I can only anticipate the demand for emergency services to
  25| grow along with it. Once again I must state the need for 24 hour staffing in the station.  Funding to
  26| maintain a minimum of two Firefighter/EMTs or Paramedics in the station 24 hours a day, 7 days a week
  27| is critical to maintain the high level of service our townspeople expect.   This would allow either the first
  28| ambulance or first piece of fire apparatus to respond immediately.  Not only would this reduce our
  29| response times to critical emergencies, it would also reduce the “wear and tear” on our Call members.
  30| This coverage would in no way reduce the need for our On Call personnel; it would only supplement their
  31| response and reduce response times.
  32| Recruitment and Retention of our Call Firefighters continues to be one of the greatest challenges
  33| for the department and is my main reason to call for more permanent staffing in the station.  Make no
  34| mistake, our Call personnel do an outstanding job, and their commitment never ceases to amaze me.
  35| However, like many Call Departments we suffer from a continual turnover in personnel.  Public Safety is
  36| a field where training and experience are critical.  A new Call Firefighter/EMT must attend over 340
  37| hours of initial training, plus additional time in orientation and field training.  Members qualifying to
  38| drive fire apparatus must complete an additional 65 hours of classroom and practical training.  On
  39| average, it takes a new member one and a half to two years to properly complete these requirements.
  40| Unfortunately, for a variety of reasons we often do not retain members for more than 5 - 6 years.
  41|                    655 Massachusetts Ave
  42| Lunenburg, MA 01462
  43|        CHIEF
  44|  Patrick A. Sullivan
  45|  TEL: (978) 582-4155
  46|  FAX: (978) 582-4033

===PAGE 86=== (plain, 49 lines)
   1| 86
   2| As an example, for the period of 2006-2016 we had a total of 52 Call Firefighters and EMTs
   3| leave the department.  In that same time we have retained 24 members, or barely half that number.  In this
   4| same time period, only one of the 5 Career Staff left, and that was a retirement.    This trend is most
   5| obvious when you look at the years of service within the organization.  At the end of 2016, 47% of our
   6| department has served for 5 years or less, 63% for 10 years or less.  Only 29% of the department has 20+
   7| years of service.  It should be noted that all of the full-time staff have 20 years or more combined call and
   8| career time with the department.
   9| Several of our personnel are members of Regional Technical Rescue units, providing services for
  10| Specialized Rescue, as well as Underwater Search and Recovery.  They stand ready to respond as needed
  11| throughout Northern Worcester County.
  12| 2016 was another active year for the Fire Prevention Office, with 508 Inspections, 13 Fire Drills
  13| and 16 Public Education initiatives performed.   By law we are required to inspect smoke and carbon
  14| monoxide detector installations when a home is sold, as well as all new oil burner and propane gas
  15| installations.  Any business holding a liquor license is also required to be inspected for fire safety issues
  16| as well.  In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans reviewed for proper fire
  17| protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process.  Personnel also continue to
  18| provide fire safety education to not only children in our schools but adults as well.    Other Fire Safety
  19| Initiatives undertaken during 2016 include:
  20| - Continuation of a Department of Fire Services Grant in the amount of $6953 to assist with
  21| Fire Safety Education in both the schools and with senior citizens.  This grant provides
  22| funding for both materials and personnel to bring fire safety education to our most vulnerable
  23| populations.  Highlights of activity through this grant include:
  24| -
  25| o Educational programs in basic fire safety in Grades K-3, including use of the
  26| Regional Fire Safety Trailer.
  27| o
  28| o Educational programs in conjunction with the Council on Aging.  These included
  29| cooking and heating safety, a CPR Demonstration and Ice safety.
  30| - Participation in the Senior Center’s “Sand for Seniors” Program.  This program provides
  31| buckets of walkway sand for senior citizens who were unable to obtain it for themselves in
  32| the winter months.  In partnership with this program, our Fire Prevention Officer Captain
  33| James Ricci delivers the sand to the residents and when possible, inspects their homes for
  34| smoke and carbon monoxide detectors.  If the resident was lacking detectors, Capt. Ricci is
  35| able to install free detectors obtained through the Senior SAFE Grant.
  36| -
  37| - It should be noted that each year, 50% of fatal fires are found to have nonfunctional or
  38| nonexistent smoke detectors.
  39| Several members are also Certified First Aid and CPR Instructors and provide training to both
  40| citizens and town employees.
  41| Our department continues to actively pursue grant funding opportunities as they occur, though
  42| those opportunities are becoming scarcer.
  43| Our department service truck was replaced in late 2016.  This one ton utility truck with plow is a
  44| multi-purpose vehicle used for snow removal, trailer towing and moving of equipment to and from
  45| emergency scenes.
  46| Apparatus replacement continues to be a concern.  Two of our front line units (Engine 4 and
  47| Ladder 1) are 21 and 29 years old years old respectively and showing their age through increasing
  48| maintenance costs and “out of service” time.  The new Engine 1 is handing a higher volume of responses
  49| to decrease the wear and tear on Engine 4, hopefully prolonging its lifespan.

===PAGE 87=== (plain, 46 lines)
   1| 87
   2|  Both units have been submitted for replacement on the FY’18 Capital Plan (Engine 4 having
   3| been turned down in FY’17) and it is hoped that due consideration will be given for their timely
   4| replacement.  The high reliability required of front line emergency apparatus becomes increasingly
   5| expensive and difficult to maintain as these units reach the end of their life span.
   6| Firefighter Frank Byrne graduated from the Massachusetts Firefighting Academy’s
   7| Call/Volunteer Training Program.  This intensive four month program provides the basic skills
   8| firefighters need to operate in today’s challenging environments.  All newly hired firefighters are required
   9| to complete this program within one year of hire.
  10| In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their
  11| outstanding commitment to the Town.  Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to
  12| impress me every day.  I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards and commissions for
  13| their continuing help.  Most people do not realize the level of interdepartmental cooperation that occurs in
  14| the daily course of town business.  My thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for their ongoing
  15| support of public safety.  It is that support that allows us to continue providing the services you expect
  16| from us.
