===PAGE 90=== (plain, 30 lines)
   1| 90
   2| Citizen’s Police Academy
   3| I would also like to thank and commend the Citizens of Lunenburg who took part in the first annual
   4| Citizen’s Police Academy. The program was a complete sensation that continues to garner an enormous
   5| amount of positive feedback.  I am very grateful for the support of the Citizen students who participated
   6| and for the generous and unselfish contributions, on the part of the Fitchburg District Court Judiciary, the
   7| Deputy Chief of the Fitchburg Police Department, and the Lunenburg Police Officers who volunteered
   8| their time, made this program a success. A special thanks to Lieutenant Mike Luth for organizing and
   9| facilitating this worthwhile endeavor.
  10| Grants
  11| The Police Department participated in the E.O.P.S. Underage Alcohol Enforcement Grant, the Highway
  12| Safety Divisions, Traffic Enforcement Grant, MIIA risk management grant, the Office of Justice
  13| Assistance Bullet Proof Vest Partnership Program, and the Department of Justice, Community Oriented
  14| Police Services Cops Hiring Program and the Commonwealth Security Trust Fund grant Program.
  15| The Underage Alcohol Enforcement Grant application was prepared by Sergeant Jack Hebert.
  16| Enforcement efforts related to the grant included compliance checks, Party Patrol, and shoulder taps. The
  17| program has been extremely successful in the past couple of years with bringing awareness to the license
  18| holders in our community as well at risk youth. The Traffic enforcement grant, which has been received
  19| by the police department for several years, is now prepared by Officer Tocci, of the traffic enforcement
  20| division. The activities include, enforcement for alcohol impaired drivers, aggressive driving and seat belt
  21| violations. The program is best known as the Click-it or Ticket program. Lieutenant Luth oversees the
  22| facilitation of these programs which have consistently met with a great deal of success.
  23| State of the Police Department
  24| The Police Department is in operation 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. The desk is open Monday through
  25| Friday from 7:00 AM to 7:00 PM.  Emergency, Non-emergency and 911 calls are routed through
  26| Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District, (NVRDD). For all emergencies, dial 911. For general calls
  27| please call (978) 582-4531.
  28| The public is reminded to report any animal-related complaints to the Regional Call Center at (978) 582-
  29| 4531. For emergencies, dial 911. More information can be found on the Animal Control website at
  30| lunenburgma.gov.

===PAGE 91=== (plain, 45 lines)
   1| 91
   2| Dogs must be licensed by April 1st annually. Dogs must have a current Rabies certificate to qualify for
   3| licensing. This can be done at the Town Clerk's office in Town Hall, 17 Main Street Lunenburg, MA
   4| 01462. Unspayed Female/Unaltered Male: $20 fee. Spayed Female/Altered Male: $5 fee.  Late Fee: $25.
   5| Patrol Officers were dispatched to 16,682 calls for service this year, investigated 300 motor vehicle
   6| accidents, investigated 1405 incidents, conducted 4034 motor vehicle stops, wrote 2714 motor vehicle
   7| violations and arrested 490 people.
   8| K-9 Jerry completed 25 weeks of continuous training in January of 2015 and over 75 maintenance
   9| training sessions to follow. Since that time the K-9 unit has been called out 90 times, deployed 80 times,
  10| and conducted 50 tracks. The K-9 unit has conducted 26 narcotics searches, which resulted in the seizure
  11| of 1.039 kilograms of narcotics and 2 handguns, and 4 evidence related searches recovering 2 handguns
  12| along with other suspect/victim property.
  13| The Desk officer answered approximately 7000 calls, fielded some 3400 lobby inquiries, wrote 94
  14| incident reports, and 6 field interview reports. This year the day shift desk watch officer was responsible
  15| for conducting suicide watch and assisting with the court processing of 112 detainees; either taken into
  16| custody overnight or during the day shift. The day shift desk officer processed 131 Firearms Licenses, and
  17| registered 13 Sex Offenders. The 3:00 PM to 7:00 PM desk watch officers assisted in the processing of 41
  18| detainees while providing suicide watch and numerous other services.
  19| Calls for service have increased 42.8%.  The arrest rate spiked last year with 636. Although that figure
  20| dropped to 490 this year, over the past five years the increase in arrests is up 66.1%. In the past five years
  21| reportable incidents, those that require an investigation, and/or court complaint, have increased by 66%,
  22| and motor vehicle accidents are up by 21.4%.
  23| Overall crime is down slightly with the exception of Group A, crimes against society. In that category
  24| weapons violations have increased. In Group B crimes; drunkenness and check fraud have increased.
  25| Group A crimes against persons; aggravated assault and sex crimes have also increased. Shoplifting and
  26| burglaries have decreased dramatically this year however; larceny, credit card fraud, stolen property, and
  27| vandalism have increased in Group A, crimes against property.
  28| Growth plays a major role in all of this data. Historically; calls for service, arrests, incidents and accidents
  29| continue to climb.  Traffic continues to increase, which too, has a direct effect on all of these categories.
  30| The demands for service will continue to rise.  As I stated in last year’s town report, the police department
  31| has been asking for an increase in staff since 1996. To prove that point I provided clips from every town
  32| report since that time.  I’ve studied other towns of similar demographics, population, commercial entities,
  33| bordering communities and found that calls for service, arrests, and incidents were remarkably higher in
  34| Lunenburg, yet those communities employed from five to eight more full-time police officers than
  35| Lunenburg.  National Law Enforcement standards call for 2 1/2 men per thousand population. That would
  36| bring the police department up to 22 full time officers. We presently have 14 including the Police Chief.
  37| The service the public expects has been steadily declining due to inadequate staffing issues over a long
  38| period of time. Again, the time to properly investigate crimes, solve problems by engaging the
  39| community, or providing a quick emergency response is continually at odds with the demands of growth.
  40| The community will eventually see the Police Department as a useless ineffective service that is a drain
  41| on their income and their support for the police will decline.  This year I am going to request that new
  42| growth fund an increase in staffing over the next several years on an incremental basis.  I respectfully ask
  43| for the public’s support for this initiative.
  44| Lunenburg is still a safe place. Despite fiscal challenges, the men and women of the Police Department
  45| remain focused and faithful to their mission.  I am proud to say, without hesitation, that every Lunenburg

===PAGE 92=== (plain, 10 lines)
   1| 92
   2| police officer works extremely hard to make Lunenburg safe and secure and provide the best service they
   3| can to all citizens who live in town, who visit or who are just passing through.
   4| Thank you again for another year. I am very proud to serve as your Police Chief. I value the support the
   5| citizens of Lunenburg have shown the Police Department. I humbly ask for your commitment as I
   6| continue seek funding for additional police officers so I can provide a level of service that you deserve
   7| and are entitled to.
   8| Respectfully Submitted,
   9| Chief James P. Marino
  10| Chief James P. Marino

   2| CAPITAL PLANNING
   3| Lunenburg’s Charter specifically mandates that a Capital Planning Committee prepare a Fiscal Year (FY)
   4| Plan and a Capital Improvement Plan. These plans are to be presented to the Town Manager.
   5| As noted in the past reports, the criterion for any capital request includes Mandates, Safety, Need, Crisis,
   6| Cost Effectiveness and Ability to Fund. The Committee has to review each request under these six
   7| elements. Not only do they have to determine that the request fits within the criteria, but they have to
   8| prioritize the level that meets the criteria. Therefore, not all requests are included in the FY Plans, mostly
   9| due to funding. The result of the proposed FY Plans provides the needed service for safety and protection
  10| of the public, the upkeep to our schools and the management of government.
  11| At the Annual Town Meeting in May 2016, the following items, totaling $ 619,475, were presented in the
  12| FY17 Plan.
  13| The Committee meets each year after the Capital Budget is presented to the Town Manager and considers
  14| improvement to the methodology of creating the budget. In future preparation the Committee will conduct
  15| more meetings on site.

   2| PLANNING BOARD
   3| Fourteen ANR plans (plans not requiring approval under the Subdivision Control Law) were approved.
   4| Approved residential subdivisions were in various stages of completion.  The following continue to build:
   5| a nine-lot subdivision on Butterfly Lane (formerly 50 Elmwood Road), Emerald Place at Lake Whalom,
   6| Stone Farm Estates off Massachusetts Avenue, and Sequoia Drive off Howard Street.  TriTown Landing
   7| off Youngs Road completed construction on Building 4 in January 2016 and all 32 units were occupied
   8| by May 2016.
   9| The Board approved Site Plan Approval applications for Hannaford-To-Go, Safelite Auto Glass, and an
  10| auto detailing business; all located on Massachusetts Avenue.  Approval was also given for a restaurant at
  11| 1 Main Street.
  12| There was an increase in the number of scenic road public hearings with the designation of two new
  13| scenic roads; Northfield Road and Flat Hill Road.  After a road has been designated as a scenic road, any
  14| repair, maintenance, reconstruction, or paving work done that involves the cutting or removal of trees
  15| within the Town way or the tearing down or destruction of stone walls requires Board approval.
  16| Lancaster Avenue and Leominster Road are also designated as scenic roads.
  17| The appearance of Electric Avenue is undergoing change with the construction of a self-storage facility
  18| and Unitil’s construction to relocate its distribution and operation center from Fitchburg to the site of the
  19| old Whalom Drive-In Theater.
  20| 2016 also saw the Planning Board revise Section 8.4. of the Protective Bylaw to a more formal Site Plan
  21| Approval bylaw which outlines a collection of objective standards to bring consistency to the review
  22| process.  Additionally, the Board adopted Rules & Regulations for filing and submissions under Section
  23| 8.4.
  24| The Citizen Planner Training Collaborative, in conjunction with the Massachusetts Department of
  25| Housing and Community Development, and the Urban Harbors Institute, University of Massachusetts
  26| Boston, conducts workshops throughout the year.  Workshops attended by Board members included
  27| Green Communities Program, USDA Rural Programs, Roles & Responsibilities of Planning & Zoning
  28| Boards (both Part 1 and 2), Introduction to Subdivision Control Law and ANR, and Planning with
  29| Community Support.  Some of those workshops are designated (MIIA) which entitles Lunenburg to
  30| credit under the MIIA Rewards Program towards public officials’ liability insurance.
  31| Board members served as representatives to the following committees:  Matthew Allison on the newly
  32| created Stormwater Task Force, Capital Planning Committee, and continued liaison to the Agricultural
  33| Commission; Damon McQuaid on the Building Reuse Committee; Gregory Bittner on the School
  34| Building Committee and Green Community Task Force; David Prokowiew as the Montachusett Regional
  35| Planning Commission representative and Open Space Committee member; and Kenneth Chenis as the
  36| Montachusett Joint Transportation Committee representative.
  37| The Planning Office is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Avenue and can be
  38| reached at 978-582-4147, extension 5.  Various forms are available online at http://lunenburgma.gov/  on
  39| the Planning Board’s homepage.  The Zoning Map, Protective Bylaws for the Town, Rules and
  40| Regulations Governing the Subdivision of Land, and Rules & Regulations for Site Plan Approval &
  41| Special Permits are available online.  The Board continues to work on updating the Protective Bylaws of
  42| the Town.

   1|                                           ZONING BOARD OF APPEALS
   2| The Zoning Board of Appeals heard sixteen petitions during calendar year 2016.   There were several appli-
   3| cants seeking special permits and special permits for dimensional variations.  The Zoning Board meetings are
   4| held on the second and fourth Wednesdays of the month on an “as needed” basis.
   5| The Zoning Board consists of the following members:  Raymond Beal, Chairman, Alfred Gravelle, Vice
   6| Chairman, James Besarkarski, Hans Wentrup and Associate members, David Blatt, Paul Doherty and Sheila
   7| Lumi.   Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
   8| The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
   9| To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town’s Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws.
  10| To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town’s Protective By-laws.
  11| To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as
  12| amended, from any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action
  13| from the Administrative Officer or Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town
  14| or other Administrative Official.
  15| To act upon application for comprehensive permits in accordance with Massachusetts General Laws, Chapter
  16| 40B.  The Zoning Board of Appeals reviews requests for comprehensive permits submitted to the Board under
  17| Chapter 40B by developers who wish to create affordable housing.  These requests can, by law, circumvent
  18| local zoning ordinances.
  19| The office of the Zoning Board of Appeals is located in the Ritter Memorial Building, 960 Massachusetts Av-
  20| enue, Lunenburg.  Business hours are 8:00 AM to 4:00 PM Monday                        and Wednesday and 8:00 AM to 6:00
  21| PM Tuesday and Thursday, closed Friday.
  22| If you need any assistance from the Zoning Board of Appeals please contact us at 978-582-4146 X 421 during
  23| regular   business   hours.      Zoning   Board   applications   are   available   in   the   office   and   online   at
  24| www.Lunenburgonline.gov.

   2| Our Vision:  The Lunenburg Public Schools prides itself on excellence.  We provide a child-centered envi-

  10|          All students have talents and can excel.

  12|          All students are capable of learning and are entitled to instructional excellence.

   1| 101
   2| Superintendent’s Message
   3|    What a remarkable year!  The District returned to a four school configuration with the opening of the
   4| new building and several necessary and attractive upgrades to existi ng buildings.  The completion of the
   5| school building project full new opportunity and possibilities elevated the excitement across the schools
   6| and community.  Our excitement and pride was surpassed only by our feeling of gratitude.
   7| Our deepest gratitude is for the voters of Lunenburg who approved the co-located middle-high school
   8| project and are paying it forward.   This is a school for the entire community and it is our earnest hope
   9| that the lives of all our citizens are enriched by this magnificent facility. We are also grateful to the
  10| citizens of the Commonwealth and the Massachusetts School Building Authority for its partnership and
  11| support of the construction.    We gathered together on October 22, 2016 for the Dedication and Ribbon
  12| Cutting Ceremony.  During the summer months over 1000 community members had toured the building.
  13| Over 200 joined us for the dedication including Congresswoman Niki Tsongas, U.S. House of
  14| Representatives, State Representative Jennifer Benson, along with members of the Board of Selectmen,
  15| School Building Committee, and the School Committee.  Students Julia Wilson, Robert Nash and
  16| Brendan Tobin spoke and did a wonderful job representing the students of our schools.  We are very
  17| proud of them.  They are our hope for the future and represent the best in us.
  18| The long awaited building was delivered on-time and under-budget.  It is everything we hoped for and our
  19| dream come true.   What a pleasant duty it was to work with everyone associated with this project.   A
  20| journey that began a decade ago resulted in a splendid modern educational facility due to the enduring
  21| dedication, hard work, vision, perseverance, and good judgement of so many.   The path was not always
  22| straight and contained its fair share of ups and downs and is the result of cooperation, teamwork, and
  23| trust.   These are the qualities we continue to embrace as we turn the page and begin a new chapter.
  24| A community’s schools hold its citizens’ hopes and dreams. It is where that which we cherish most, our
  25| children, learn and grow.  Like our homes, schools are places where our children are nurtured and
  26| supported; where children learn to trust and to be trustworthy.  Our schools must ever be places where
  27| kindness and compassion are in ample supply, freely given and where ideas matter and learning is
  28| meaningful.  The new building represents a commitment that for generations to come the children of the
  29| Lunenburg community will always find a place where hope abides and where dreams are made.

   1| 102
   2| The Jane Doyle Community Playground dedication was held on Wednesday, November 23, 2016.  This
   3| new playground is located by the Turkey Hill Elementary School Gymnasium.  The playground was made
   4| possible by the generous donations and support from the Connor Lourens Memorial Fund; the Arnold
   5| Family and BEIM Foundation; Turkey Hill Family Lions Club; Lunenburg PTO; the Extended Day
   6| Program; and many Lunenburg parents and community members.  In addition to the playground,
   7| basketball courts with areas for the very popular game “four-square” were constructed.    We appreciate
   8| everyone who made these wonderful, age-appropriate play areas possible.
   9| The Lunenburg School Committee contracted with New England Education Consultants (NEEC) to
  10| facilitate a strategic visioning and planning initiative this year.  The initial step in this work is a creating a
  11| Strategic Framework  consisting of a Mission, Core Values, and Vision, all of which serve to set direction
  12| for the rest of the planning process.  The Strategic Framework is developed members of a Steering
  13| Committee working with internal and external stakeholders to help assure the values of the community
  14| are well represented and that a connection is made to the public regarding underlying principles for the
  15| planning process.  The new framework and plan will be adopted by the School Committee by the end of
  16| the school year with implementation to begin in the fall.    With the opening of the new Lunenburg
  17| Middle –High School, the Lunenburg School Committee also committed to develop and implement an
  18| adult education program for the benefit of the Lunenburg community. An advisory group was appointed
  19| by the School Committee to research and make recommendations by the end of the current school year.
  20| Brendan Ryan Tobin was the 2016 Superintendent’s Award recipient.  Brendan is the son of Thomas and
  21| Carolyn Tobin.  During his four years at LHS Brendan excelled in honors and advanced placement
  22| coursework.   He has been the President of the Class of 2017 since 9th grade.  Brendan is a poised,
  23| humble, and well-spoken young man who represented his school and classmates extremely well.  He was
  24| honored at the Worcester County Superintendent's Roundtable Scholars' ceremony in January.  He
  25| selected the following quote from Colin Powell for that ceremony's program.  “Success is the result of
  26| perfection, hard work, learning from failure, loyalty, and persistence.”
  27| This past year our schools bid farewell to retirees who devoted many years to public service.  Retiring this
  28| past year were:  Steve Archambault, Middle School Music Teacher; Rhonda Malatos, High School
  29| Physical Education Teacher; Judy Haley, Middle School Cafeteria Manager; Linda Papadopoulos, Middle
  30| School Special Education Teacher; and Natalie Davulis, Middle School Grade 7 Teacher.  When added
  31| together these employees have 116 years of service to our students, schools, and community.  I hope the
  32| benefit our children derived from their dedication, compassion, and hard work is repaid in kind with long,
  33| happy and healthy retirements sprinkled with memories of fulfilled careers. On behalf of our school
  34| community, I extend to each of them our heartfelt thanks and sincere appreciation for their service.
  35| In closing I want to acknowledge and extend special thanks to the PTO, our parents/guardians and the
  36| community members who support the PTO.  This year the PTO donated $32,000 to the schools to support
  37| a variety of opportunities like field trips and educational speakers that are not provided within the school
  38| budget.   The PTO has a tradition of active engagement and service.  The students, faculty, and staff of
  39| our schools share this tradition of service to others.  It is vital to our mission and our students’ future
  40| success in life.    We are very grateful to the entire Lunenburg community for their support of our students
  41| and schools.
  42| Respectfully Submitted,
  43| Loxi Jo Calmes, Superintendent of Schools

===PAGE 103=== (plain, 37 lines)
   1| 103
   2|  School Facilities
   3| Construction of the new Middle -High School (Phase 1) concluded by late May 2016. Shawmut Design and
   4| Construction executed the building project with NV5 providing owner’s project management services. The
   5| School Building Committee continues to provide construction and financial oversight. Phase 2 began shortly
   6| after the school year ended with removing all furniture from the former high school, relocating all teacher
   7| supplies and equipment to the new school, removal of asbestos in the former high school, then demolition,
   8| construction of the new greenhouse and two athletic fields, re -working the student parking lot, tennis courts,
   9| construction of an outdoor basketball court and remaining site work. Work was nearly complete by late De-
  10| cember 2016. A minor amount of work remains to be completed in the spring of 2017 such as the finish sur-
  11| face of the tennis courts, basketball court and installation of an athletic field scoreboard.
  12| In February 2016, the Town of Lunenburg released a Request for Proposals for rooftop solar systems to be
  13| installed on the new Middle-High School and Department of Public Works garage. The solar array was to be
  14| a 20 year agreement that rented roof space and allowed the school district to purchase the electric power at a
  15| discounted rate. Solect Energy was awarded the contract to install a 349 kilowatt system that would generate
  16| 394,000 KWH per year and would be purchased at a fixed rate of 8.3 cents per kilowatt. Construction began
  17| in November 2016 and is expected to be complete by early spring 2017.
  18| The focus of school maintenance for FY17 continues to be preventative maintenance of heating and ventila-
  19| tion systems and energy conservation measures. In addition, the maintenance staff began training on the
  20| building systems of the new school in July.  Conversion of existing interior lighting to LED at Lunenburg
  21| Primary School began in December 2016 and will complete in March 2017.  Funding for conversion of Tur-
  22| key Hill Elementary School will be a high priority in the upcoming Green Communities Competitive Grant
  23| rounds.
  24| The third grade was relocated to Turkey Hill, which was re-designated an elementary school, in June 2016.
  25| To be better able to support grades 3-5, a playground was added to the side of the school by the generous do-
  26| nation of the Connor Lourens Memorial Fund, the BEIM Foundation c/o Erik and Megan Arnold, the Turkey
  27| Hill Family Lions Club, the Lunenburg PTO, Extended Day Program, parents, guardians, and citizens. The
  28| playground was dedicated in September 2016 in memory of Jane Doyle.
  29| The FY 17 capital program funded replacement of the Turkey Hill Elementary School phone system and in-
  30| tercom system at $22,000 each. The new systems were installed by Vertical Communications Inc. in July
  31| 2016. For the FY18 Capital Program, the School Department has requested an additional $145,000 funding
  32| for continued asbestos removal at Turkey Hill Elementary School as its highest priority.  Additionally the
  33| district is looking to add a security camera system to Turkey Hill Elementary School and Lunenburg Primary
  34| School during FY17 using funds that are anticipated to be surplus to the operating budget due to reduced cost
  35| for natural gas and electricity and the impact of the solar projects.
  36| Respectfully submitted,
  37| John Londa, Director of Facilities and Grounds

===PAGE 104=== (layout, 35 lines)
   1|                                                   Technology Department
   2| It has been a great pleasure to oversee the stunning technology improvements to the district over the past
   3| year.  Bringing the new Lunenburg Middle-High School online has provided not only a major upgrade to
   4| technology for our sixth through twelfth grade classes, but has afforded us the opportunity to expand access
   5| to devices and improve infrastructure at the Primary and Elementary schools as well.  IPad carts, Wi-Fi ac-
   6| cess points, desktops, laptops, printers and other technology were redistributed from the previous Lunenburg
   7| High School to the Primary and Elementary schools, improving each of those areas by as much as fifty per-
   8| cent.
   9| The new Middle-High School is home to state of the art technology that is pervasive throughout the building.
  10|     Interactive projectors and voice-lift audio systems are in all classrooms and collaborative workspaces.
  11|     Grades ten through twelve are participating in a one-to-one Chromebook initiative, putting a device in
  12|      each student’s hands for use in class and at home.
  13|     Computer labs, both mobile and static, comprise more than two hundred additional devices for use on the
  14|      premises.
  15|     A high capacity Wi-Fi system with more than one hundred access points provides ubiquitous, secure ac-
  16|      cess to the network for students, faculty, and staff – even allowing for multiple devices per person.
  17|     A beautiful new auditorium space with professionally designed sound, lights and rigging is available for
  18|      use not only by students and school personnel but for town functions and outside productions and events
  19|      as well.
  20| The newly named Turkey Hill Elementary School has seen some significant technology improvements this
  21| past year.  The number of Wi-Fi access points was increased by fifty percent, improving coverage and capac-
  22| ity throughout the building.  Additional iPad and Chromebook carts have been deployed, some from the pre-
  23| vious Lunenburg High School and some donated by extremely generous individuals and organizations.  Ad-
  24| ditional multimedia carts were also deployed providing projection and audio capabilities to more classrooms.
  25| Lunenburg Primary School also benefited from the closing of LHS.  Again, we were able to increase the
  26| number of Wi-Fi access points by fifty percent, deploy additional mobile computer labs, replace outdated
  27| classroom computers, and increase the availability of multimedia carts.
  28| The Technology Department is also proud to have added an additional full-time staff member, our School
  29| Technology Coordinator Nick Scoggins.  Our goal is always to use technology to enhance our students’
  30| learning experience, and to get that technology into as many students’ hands as possible.  This past year has
  31| provided us with an opportunity to do that on a grander scale than ever before.
  32| Respectfully submitted,
  33| Steve Malandrinos
  34| Technology Director

   1|                                                 Special Services Department
   2|                                    FY16 (data taken from 2015-2016 school year)
   3|           From the March 1, 2016 report, there are two hundred and ninety nine students with disabilities re-
   4| ceiving special education or related services in Lunenburg.  This represents 18.8% of our total student popula-
   5| tion of 1,587 students (it was 17.2% last year).  The state average from the 2016 school report card is 17.2%.
   6| As of March 1, 2016, the total number of students receiving services outside the district was nineteen (last
   7| year was thirty).  Of the nineteen students, four are placed in Collaborative Placements, twelve are in Day
   8| Placements and three are in Residential Placements.  Additionally, as of January 12, 2017, one hundred four-
   9| teen students identified with a disability received accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg.
  10|           This year as a district, our overall Accountability and Assistance Level is Level 2 with the Primary
  11| School, Turkey Hill Middle School, and Lunenburg High School all being listed as Level 2 (LHS was Level 1
  12| last year).  The state report card lists the district’s determination of need for Special Education technical assis-
  13| tance or intervention for Students with Disabilities in Lunenburg as “Meets Requirements – At Risk
  14| (MRAR)”. The district’s Students with Disabilities progress towards narrowing proficiency gaps [Cumulative
  15| Progress and Performance Index (CPI): 1-100)] is listed at 45 (it was 46 last year) with the target set for 75.
  16| Students with Disabilities participated in MCAS this year with the following rates:  English Language Arts =
  17| 99% (it was 99% last year), Mathematics = 100% (It was 99% last year), and Science = 100% (It was 100%
  18| last year).

  20| linkid=30&orgcode=01620000&orgtypecode=5&

  23| certain Indicators they are targeting each year.  For our Indicators, the following information is available be-
  24| low and also can be found at: http://profiles.doe.mass.edu/gis/sped_map.aspx?orgcode=01620000&

  26| was 82.0%. Lunenburg’s rate was 66.7% and the overall state rate was 69.9% for Students with Disabilities.
  27| Indicator 2 – Dropout Rate.  For the 2014-2015 school year the state target for Students with Disabilities was
  28| 3.0%. Lunenburg rate for Students with IEPs was 0% (it was 2.2% last year) and the overall state rate was
  29| 2.9%.

   1| Indicator 4 – Suspension (more than 10 days)/Expulsion for Students with IEPs.  For Lunenburg, our rate
   2| was 0% (last year it was .7%.  The state rate was 1.1%.   Additionally, Indicator 4B records significant dis-
   3| crepancies with the Suspension/Expulsion rates by race or ethnicity.  In 2015, Lunenburg was not found to
   4| have any significant discrepancies due to these factors.
   5| Indicator 5 - Educational Environments for Students Aged 6-21 with IEPs. For 2014-2015, the state’s target
   6| rates are as follows:  for Full Inclusion of students = 60.5%, Partial Inclusion = N/A, Substantially Separate
   7| Placements = 14.5%, and for Separate Schools/Residential Facilities/Homebound/Hospitals = 5.5%.  In
   8| Lunenburg, our percentages are as follows:  Full Inclusion = 69.6, Partial = 13.2%, Substantially Separate =
   9| 7.0% and Separate schools, Residential, etc. = 10.1%.
  10| Indicator 6 – Educational Environments for Students Aged 3-5 with IEPs.  The 2014-2015 state rates
  11| showed the percent of students receiving a majority of their special education and related services in a Full
  12| Inclusion early childhood program was 41.0%, in a Partial Inclusion Program was N/A, a Substantially Sepa-
  13| rate Placement (special education class, separate school, or residential facility) was 13.5%, and students not
  14| attending an early childhood program but receiving only a related service (e.g. speech, OT, PT) was N/A. In
  15| Lunenburg, our early childhood Full Inclusion rate was 32.4%, our Partial Inclusion rate was 44.1%, Substan-
  16| tially Separate rate was 17.6%, and Related Services was 5.9%.
  17| Indicator 8 – Parent Involvement.  This indicator measures the % of parents with a child receiving special
  18| education who report that school facilitated parent involvement as a means of improving services and results
  19| for students with IEPs.  For 2014-2015 the state target was 85%.  Lunenburg’s rate was 78.3% and the state’s
  20| rate was 79.7% (from information collected in the spring of 2013).
  21| Indicator 9 & 10 - Both of these Indicators deal with Disproportionality in Special Education and in Special
  22| Education Categories.  In Lunenburg the state determined that there was no disproportionality found in either
  23| case.
  24| Indicator 11 – Initial Evaluation Timelines.   This indicator measures the % of children with parental consent
  25| to evaluate, who were evaluated within the State established timeline. Data is reported for all initial evalua-
  26| tions initiated in October, November, and December of the reporting year.  Lunenburg was rated 100% in
  27| compliance and the state average for this Indicator was 98.7%.
  28| Indicator 12 – Early Childhood Transition.  This indicator measures the % of children referred by Part C,
  29| found eligible for special education services, and who have an IEP developed and implemented by their third
  30| birthdays. The state target for this indicator in 100% and Lunenburg is noted as “Suppressed”.  Data is sup-
  31| pressed if the number of students referred by Part C and found eligible for special education services is fewer
  32| than 10.  In Lunenburg, only 2 students were referred during 2012-2013 (we have not been reviewed since
  33| 2012-2013 for updated data in this area).
  34| Indicator 13 – Secondary Transition.  This indicator measures the % of youth with IEPs aged 16 and above
  35| with an IEP that includes appropriate measurable postsecondary goals that are annually updated and based
  36| upon an age appropriate transition assessment, transition services, including courses of study, that will rea-
  37| sonably enable the student to meet those postsecondary goals, and annual IEP goals related to the student's
  38| transition service needs. Lunenburg was rated 100% in compliance and the state average for this Indicator
  39| was 99.2% (we have not been reviewed since 2011-2012 for updated data in this area).
  40| Indicator 14 – Post-School Outcomes for Students with IEPs.  This indicator measures the % of students
  41| with IEPs who exited high school during the 2013-14 school year and self-reported post-school engagement
  42| in education or employment one year after leaving high school. Engagement is defined through three meas-
  43| urements:
  44|     Measurement A: Enrolled in higher education within one year of leaving high school.
  45|     Measurement B: Enrolled in higher education or competitively employed within one year of leaving high
  46| school.
  47|     Measurement C: Enrolled in higher education, or in some other postsecondary education or training pro-
  48| gram; or competitively employed, or in some other employment within one year of leaving high school.
  49| Measurements are cumulative with Measurement C reflecting the total number of exiters engaged in a post-
  50| secondary activity one year after leaving high school. For the 2014-2015 school year state targets for the
  51| three measurements of Post-School Outcomes are Measurement A: 47%; Measurement B: 82%; and
  52|                                                                   106

===PAGE 108=== (layout, 51 lines)
   1|                                         LUNENBURG PRIMARY SCHOOL
   2|                                                                                                                                    nd
   3|          The Lunenburg Primary School is an early childhood education center for students Preschool to 2
   4| grade.  Our students range from three to eight years old. Our community is made up of students, parents,
   5| teachers and support staff; working together to create an educationally rich and safe learning environment that
   6| focuses on the whole child.
   7|          The October 1, 2016 student count reported to the Department of Elementary and Secondary Educa-
   8| tion was three hundred and eighty–six students.  Our Preschool program services forty-one three and four year
   9| old students. We have five Kindergarten classes servicing one hundred and eighteen students. Class sizes in
  10| our Kindergartens are at 23 to 24 students.  First grade has one hundred and eleven students with class sizes
  11| ranging from 22 to 23. Second grade has one hundred and sixteen students with class sizes ranging from 23 to
  12| 24 students.  Students and staff this year are enjoying the extra space produced when the third grade students
  13| and staff were moved to the Turkey Hill Elementary School as a result of the new Lunenburg Middle-High
  14| School build.
  15|          Our professional development this year centered on Everyday Math Online Resources, Responsive
  16| Classroom and Emotional Intelligence. All of our classroom teachers are working on improving the use of
  17| Responsive Classroom Learning Curriculum which includes morning meetings, guided discovery, rules, logi-
  18| cal consequences and academic choice.  Two years ago we updated our math curriculum and materials to
  19| Every Day Math.  Teachers have been involved in two years of professional development activities focused
  20| on lesson structure, math games and developing ways to incorporate technology into their teaching of math.
  21| This year students and parents were given access to the online resources as well so that students and parents
  22| have a place to explore math concepts and access games on line to practice math skills.
  23|          To strengthen our commitment to focusing on the whole child and the Social Emotional Learning
  24| (SEL) needs of our children, teachers and staff have received professional development on Responsive class-
  25| room and Emotional Intelligence.  Mrs. Nancy Chapdelaine has led our educators through in-service lessons
  26| dedicated to Understanding the Power of Our Words and Morning Meeting structures and strategies. After st
  27| attending the Yale Institutes RULER Training, Mrs. Dorsey; School Psychologist, Mrs. Favreau; 1                              Grade
  28| Teacher and Mrs. Blaisdell, Principal, led teachers through completing PD hours focused on Emotional Intel-
  29| ligence using RULER.  The PD activities have focused just on the staff understanding RULER and learning
  30| how to utilize the tools to understand their own emotions and how they affect our work together with children.
  31| This is the beginning of a three to four year commitment to making the Lunenburg Primary School one that
  32| focuses on helping our community of students, staff and parents understand their emotions and how we bring
  33| our best selves to the learning we do each and every day.  Information on these two SEL’s can be found at
  34| https://www.responsiveclassroom.org/ and http://ei.yale.edu/
  35|          The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to
  36| insure that all students have the services they need to be successful. Common assessments are completed in
  37| the fall, winter and spring for all children in grades K-3 in the areas of reading, writing, and math.  These
  38| common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and drive individual student
  39| instruction.  Struggling students are identified quickly and additional resources and instruction are provided to
  40| address the areas of concern identified.  Tiered instructional services are provided in the area of Language,
  41| Arts and Math.  The tiered instructional model requires that the teachers not only meet the needs of the stu-
  42| dents who may be struggling with learning but those students who are doing well and need to achieve higher
  43| levels of learning.  Learning is more personalized and students are being challenged to be confident independ-
  44| ent learners and problem solvers.
  45| Our staff works hard to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and supportive of each of our
  46| members. We have high expectations, insist on respectful behavior, and recognize the importance of building
  47| a community where members feel a sense of belonging and responsibility to contribute.  Our students have
  48| maintained a high level of community engagement and civic learning that is fostered through service learning
  49| projects at each grade level.  The Kindergarten students learn about our local food bank and gift mart and
  50| sponsor school-wide can and gift drives through-out the year.  Our
  51|                                                                 108

===PAGE 109=== (layout, 22 lines)
   1|          2nd grade students have learned about our local veterans and organized Valentines to go to area Vet-
   2| eran’s Hospitals.  Students in the First grade have committed their energy to support our local animal shel-
   3| ters.
   4|          The Primary School Parent/Teacher Advisory Council meets each month to discuss school issues,
   5| review the annual budget for the school and advise administration on the school’s parent handbook and to
   6| develop the School Improvement Plan.
   7| Members of the Council for the 2016-2017 school year were as follows:
   8| Principal:                               Elaine E. Blaisdell

  11| Parents:                                 Kristen Burns, John Eberly, Jared Perrine
  12| Community Representative:         Julie Bertram
  13| School Committee Rep:                    Dawn-Marie Ayles
  14|          The faculty and staff of the Primary School are extremely proud of the work we do with our children.
  15| We are deeply committed to student learning, our own life-long learning and improving learning.  Only
  16| through working with parents as partners are we able to accomplish our goals.  For more information about
  17| the work that we do at the Lunenburg Primary School please visit our website:                        https://sites.google.com/a/

   2| TURKEY HILL ELEMENTARY SCHOOL
   3| With the completion of the new Middle High school, Turkey Hill has been reconfigured as an elementary
   4| school for students in grades 3 -5. The staff includes a number of skilled veterans who have been reunited from
   5| T.C. Passios days as well as many young and energetic new educators.  We are focused on developing our own
   6| distinctive perspective as to how we best prepare students for lifelong learning and responsible community
   7| membership.
   8| Our academic priority is to increase student learning and improve students’ ability to think critically and
   9| achieve at high levels.  The school advisory council has grappled with how we might best reflect progress par-
  10| ticularly focusing this year on math, using MCAS data. We agreed to reflect growth using three measures:
  11|  Increase by 3 points annually the CPI score in Math
  12|  Increase to 70% the % of Proficient or Higher in Math
  13|  Attain a two year average of 50% SGP in Math by the end of 5th grade

===PAGE 112=== (plain, 52 lines)
   1| 112
   2| For some staff, professional development has focused on the Everyday Math Program, as the most recent ver-
   3| sion has radically altered the scope and sequence of curriculum to more accurately reflect the demands of the
   4| Common Core. Other teachers have taken on extra responsibilities as part of the ELA, Math, and Science task
   5| forces, in order to create comprehension curriculum maps for grades K -12.  We have embraced and continue
   6| to refine the Teacher Support Team protocol, which examines struggling students’ academic/personal histo-
   7| ries and focuses on the best interventions. Our aim is not only to support a teacher’s current concerns, but also
   8| to streamline access to important background information and most successful strategies for next year’s teach-
   9| ers.   Staff is learning to access Edwin Analytics, the Dept. of Education’s data warehouse, so they might ana-
  10| lyze the individual MCAS scores of their current students and more precisely respond to areas of strength or
  11| weakness.  All students were assessed in October and will be again in May using the Developmental Reading
  12| Assessment (DRA). It is a designed to help teachers systematically observe, record, and evaluate changes in
  13| student reading performance and features tools to assess reading engagement, fluency, and comprehension.
  14| We are fortunate to have the services of a full time reading specialist, who works with Title 1 students and
  15| provides support and consultation for classroom teachers. Finally, we also have a wide variety of academical-
  16| ly themed field trips planned, both out in the community and several “in -house” science and fine arts pro-
  17| grams.
  18| We acknowledge that social learning in a supportive community is as important to success as academic learn-
  19| ing.  To that end, grades 3 and 4 use the Second Step guidance curriculum, and grade 5 has developed a set of
  20| lessons that emphasize executive functioning skills.  With the full time services of our guidance counselor,
  21| many students meet weekly in small groups to develop both social skills and strategies to cope with anxiety,
  22| focus issues, and academic challenges.  We facilitate the opportunity for outside counselors to meet with stu-
  23| dents one to one. We host the Big Sibs, Little Sibs program on Wednesday afternoons for high school students
  24| to make connections with younger students in a structured activity group.  We offer a variety of after school
  25| activities so students might continue to develop their social adeptness and sense of self confidence.  We have
  26| built time into our faculty meetings for teachers to send every single student a postcard once during the year,
  27| celebrating their strengths, developing skills, or positive attributes.  We hosted the TIGER program from
  28| Plymouth State University that focuses on a positive message of inclusiveness and how to advocate for your-
  29| self and others.  We have developed a set of standard expectations for hallway behavior, playground behavior,
  30| and cafeteria behavior.
  31| We are engaged in this transition with a foundation of open communication and sound operational practices.
  32| Our leadership team consists of reps from each grade level, special areas, and special services. We are focused
  33| on developing the attributes of a highly effective team, as our ultimate goal is to model those attributes and
  34| see them mirrored in every other “team” throughout the building. We place strong value on turn-taking, empa-
  35| thy, embracing the difficult conversations, and psychological safety. The members are both conduits of com-
  36| munication between administration and staff and decision makers about the direction of our school’s culture
  37| and priorities.  Likewise, the School Advisory Council, composed of parents, community members, teachers
  38| and a school committee representative, also provide input and influence the school’s culture and priorities.
  39| The principal’s role as instructional leader is part of the equation as well. Providing effective feedback to
  40| teachers around the teaching and learning is an essential element for student success.
  41| Education is a community partnership.  Ultimately, THES does not stand in isolation. It is the sustenance of
  42| the larger community that enriches the learning opportunities for students and staff. The list of supporters is
  43| long and likely incomplete: the PTO whose funds support a huge variety of endeavors, the Lunenburg Lions
  44| Club who has been so instrumental in the creation of our beautiful playground, an anonymous donor who gift-
  45| ed  an entire Chromebook cart, BoxTop collectors and counters, Hannaford Helps supporters, Community
  46| Reader day volunteers, parents (both those who support their children at home as well as those who donate
  47| hours to school), the Rebecca’s Lodge who donated dictionaries to third grade, the Lunenburg Cultural Coun-
  48| cil whose grant makes it possible for our African Arts in Education program, our Veteran’s Day and Memorial
  49| Day guests and guest speakers.  Their selfless efforts exemplify what we strive to imbue in our children:   re-
  50| sponsible community membership.
  51| Respectfully Submitted,
  52| Heidi Champagne, Principal

===PAGE 114=== (plain, 26 lines)
   1| 114
   2| Lunenburg Middle School
   3| Lunenburg Middle School provides a rigorous academic curriculum as well as strong programs that support
   4| students’ social and emotional growth. These programs ensure that each child becomes a self-motivated,
   5| creative, lifelong learner in a safe, stimulating environment that encourages diversity and open honest com-
   6| munication.   The dedicated and accomplished staff of professionals within the school embraces the philos-
   7| ophy of educating the whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This philosophy is a means by
   8| which all students become positive community members that possess the skills to be successful in high
   9| school. Lunenburg Middle School is in its first year as a co-located middle- high school serving grades six
  10| through eight in a beautiful new facility. Middle school students have experienced some new opportunities
  11| with the new middle school, such as foreign language for grades seven and eight and increased technology
  12| instruction from a dedicated technology teacher. Grade six students have an increase in special area oppor-
  13| tunities, such as health, art music and guidance. We will continue to seek out opportunities for students that
  14| foster critical thinking and problem solving skills that prepare them for high school and beyond.
  15| In reviewing the results of the 2016 Spring M.C.A.S. tests, our percentage of students scoring in the ad-
  16| vanced and proficient range remains strong. The Department of Elementary and Secondary Education did
  17| not provide 2016 state percentages of students scoring in the proficient and advanced range due to a number
  18| of districts participating in the PARCC assessments. State percentages from 2015 have been provided mere-
  19| ly as a reference. In five of the six content areas that have student growth measured, Lunenburg Middle
  20| School students performed in the 50% or higher as a grade level median, indicating average to above aver-
  21| age student growth.  Although we are proud of our students’ performance, we continue to analyze data in
  22| our monthly results faculty meetings designed to improve our instruction for all students. Our faculty has
  23| identified areas of improvement based upon the data and implemented strategies to improve their instruc-
  24| tional practices that will help students succeed.
  25| This is the first year in four years in which the eighth grade has returned to the traditional middle school
  26| model from the high school. As the eighth grade transitions back to a true middle school model, we reflect

===PAGE 115=== (plain, 38 lines)
   1| 115
   2| on strategies that have been successful with students they experienced in the high school setting, while continu-
   3| ing to incorporate the foundations of a successful middle school. This integration of strategies continues to have
   4| a positive impact on the students we serve and we will continue to refine and implement what is best for stu-
   5| dents.
   6| We continue to utilize intervention and power blocks during the school day to meet the needs of each individual
   7| student. These blocks of instructional time that have been built into our master schedule allow teachers time to
   8| work with students at their own individual skill level.  The philosophy is to challenge students who are high
   9| academic achievers as well as strengthen skills for students who have identified weaknesses. Teachers are able
  10| to work with students in a small group to answer individual questions or provide more individualized support.
  11| We design this support model to allow students to feel more confident, thus, perform better in the classroom.
  12| We will continue to refine our intervention block times to best meet the needs of our students.
  13| Lunenburg Middle School continues to offer enrichment blocks for all students. Enrichment classes provide
  14| students a block of time in which they receive instruction in the areas of developmental health and guidance.
  15| Students also receive instruction in physical education, art, and music classes that allows them to explore their
  16| strengths and talents. Students also have the opportunity to sign up for and participate in band and chorus dur-
  17| ing this block. This enrichment block is in addition to the traditional special area classes students receive, and
  18| this block offers students additional physical movement throughout the day, additional opportunities for hands
  19| on activities and more exposure to the arts, theatre and technology throughout the school day.
  20| We make a positive impact on the students we serve by offering a variety of different programs developed to
  21| promote social and emotional growth, self-awareness, and community service. We will continue to utilize De-
  22| velopmental Design and Advisory programs to deliver our social emotional curriculum. Students meet with
  23| teachers in small groups to teach social responsibility and problem solving, as well as developing empathy and
  24| promoting positive self-esteem. Our seventh grade students are participating in themes such as embracing and
  25| respecting diversity.  All of the social emotional programs have been developed to allow students to showcase
  26| their talents and abilities, grow and improve as individuals, and promote community outreach for the younger
  27| generation.
  28| This year, we have been able to offer foreign language back to our seventh and eighth grades for the first time
  29| in several years. The addition of the foreign language instruction has been well received and the students enjoy
  30| exploring different cultures and languages.
  31| Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide educational services and support that ensures student success
  32| and promotes lifelong learning and responsible community membership. The key components to our success is
  33| our talented faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community who sup-
  34| port our teaching and learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learn-
  35| ing. Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.
  36| Respectfully submitted,
  37| Tim Santry
  38| Principal

   1| 117
   2| Lunenburg High School
   3| Lunenburg High School has been a hub for academic and social activity in 2016-2017. We have made the
   4| transition to the new Lunenburg Middle-High School.  The transition process was far bigger than just mov-
   5| ing from one building to another.  With the middle and high school in the same building, there have been far
   6| more opportunities than challenges.  High school students have been positive influences on the middle
   7| school students and the staffs of the middle and high schools now have the chance to engage in vertical team-
   8| ing.  All students in grades 10-12 have been issued a Chrome Book and there are wireless capabilities
   9| throughout the building.  Every classroom and instructional space has an interactive projector and voice lift
  10| capabilities. Our students are fortunate to be in a building that can meet the needs of the 21st century learner.
  11| This year, we have gone back to the traditional high school model.  Grades 9-12 are part of the high school
  12| and grades 6-8 are part of the middle school.  Besides the bus ride into school, there is very limited contact
  13| between high school and middle school students.  We have two lunches comprised of high school students
  14| and two lunches with only middle school students.  Additionally, the building was designed to have separate
  15| locker rooms for middle and high school students.   Overall, there have been very few issues with keeping
  16| the schools separate.  As a matter of fact, we will likely look for more opportunities to bring the students to-
  17| gether, in the future.
  18| NEASC ACCREDITATION
  19| Lunenburg High School completed a Special Progress Report for the Commission on Public Secondary
  20| Schools of the New England Association of Schools and Colleges.  In 2010, the Commission recommended
  21| that the school receive continued accreditation in the Association. The Commission's decision was based up-
  22| on review of an evaluation report prepared by a visiting committee in April 2010.  The Two Year Progress
  23| Report, submitted in January, 2013 received high marks from the Commission reviewing our submission.
  24| The Mid-Cycle Progress Report was reviewed and accepted by the Commission.  A Special Progress Report
  25| was filed in September to certify that the building project had been completed.  Lunenburg High School has
  26| been taken off “Warning” status and given full accreditation.
  27| SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
  28| Throughout the year, Lunenburg High School students are involved in a multitude of fundraising and collec-
  29| tion endeavors. In the fall, a canned goods drive was held and admission to the PowderPuff game was two
  30| canned goods.  Everything collected was donated to the Lunenburg Food Pantry.  Around the holiday season,
  31| advisories collected items for the Lunenburg Gift Mart.
  32| There is a Senior English class running this year that has a community service learning component.  Students
  33| were tasked with researching and identifying needs in the community and then forming an action plan to ad-
  34| dress the needs.  For example, we have students working on: a pet shelter drive, creating trail maps for
  35| Lunenburg conservation land, bringing a small breakfast program to LHS, and forming a group to inform
  36| and educate about mental health issues.
  37| Lunenburg High School received a generous donation in the amount of $500 from Mr. Mike’s Mobil in
  38| Lunenburg, MA.  The grant is to be used toward STEM initiatives at the high school.  We are in the process
  39| of purchasing a new robot for our Robotics class.
  40| Hannaford Helps Schools made a generous donation to Lunenburg High School of $500 from receipt sales.
  41| This money will be used to benefit a variety of programs at LHS.
  42| In March, the National Honor Society will be having its annual Blood Drive.  Students sixteen and over (and
  43| staff members) will be able to participate in the Blood Drive with parent permission.
  44| LHS has held evening presentations for parents/guardians on the following topics:

===PAGE 118=== (layout, 43 lines)
   1|    Planning for High School (grade 8)

   5| Financing your Education/Financial Aid (grades 8-12)
   6| LHS hosted 40 college admissions counselors in the school counseling office to visit with LHS students.  In
   7| addition, we held two “Decision Day” programs with Fitchburg State University and Mount Wachusett Com-
   8| munity College.
   9| MCAS RESULTS
  10| We are pleased to announce that Lunenburg High School is recognized by the Department of Elementary and
  11| Secondary Education as a Level 2 school.  As a Level 2 school, we continue to be among the highest perform-
  12| ing schools in the Commonwealth, but shows that we are not meeting our gap narrowing goals.  While we are
  13| recognized as a high performing school, we understand that there is work to be done in showing growth with
  14| our high needs population.
  15|                               Lunenburg High School - 2016 MCAS Results (Grade 10)
  16|              Subject Test                          Advanced/Proficient                     State Average (Adv/Pro)
  17|                   ELA                                         99%                                        91%
  18|                   Math                                        90%                                        78%
  19|         Science/Technology                                    92%                                        73%
  20| When you combine the percentage bands of the advanced and proficient range, LHS tenth grade students
  21| scored 99% on the ELA test.  In Mathematics, tenth grade students scored 90% advanced/proficient. In Sci-
  22| ence/Technology/Engineering, tenth grade students scored 92% advanced/proficient.  We are extremely proud
  23| of our students and teachers for their continued improvement, and we will work to move all students to the lev-
  24| el of proficiency as measured by MCAS and our goal of college and career readiness as they move through our
  25| curriculum.
  26| We were also very pleased to learn that 30 seniors at Lunenburg High School received the state’s John and Abi-
  27| gail Adams Scholarship this year.  The scholarship is available to students whose MCAS performance puts
  28| them at the top 25 percent of their district.  To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam
  29| and Proficient on the other, or Advanced on both the English and Math exams.
  30| HOMECOMING, ATHLETICS AND EXTRACURRICULAR ACTIVITIES
  31| Homecoming was once again a great town-wide celebration, culminating with a beautiful parade complete with
  32| floats representing all four high school classes.  Due to weather concerns, Homecoming was moved one week
  33| and we were able to play on our new turf field.  For much of the fall season, our home games needed to be
  34| played off-site (i.e. Doyle Field, Progin Park in Lancaster, and Marshall Park.)  Many of our sports teams quali-
  35| fied for tournament and district play.  The LHS Mock Trial team has performed well this year, going 1-1 in lo-
  36| cal trials.  The Latin Club, marching band, jazz band and the math team represented LHS in numerous competi-
  37| tions; the 51th annual Competitive Class Plays delighted the audience, and The Pops and Senior Showcase are
  38| scheduled for the spring.
  39| COMMUNITY BUILDING
  40| National Honor Society successfully supported the “Giftmart” drive during the holidays, helping to provide joy
  41| to needy children and families in conjunction with local agencies.  The World Hunger Task Force will be rais-
  42| ing money through Penny Wars this spring.  The money will be donated toward the fight against hunger.
  43|                                                                 118

===PAGE 119=== (plain, 43 lines)
   1| 119
   2| In December, we brought the SHINE Initiative, to LHS.   Three members of the SHINE Initiative gave
   3| presentations to students in all grade levels.  The presentations were about 45 minutes long. The SHINE Ini-
   4| tiative focuses on the mental health and wellness of children and young adults.  They raise awareness of
   5| mental illness and help to defuse and debunk some of the misconceptions and myths that have fueled stigma
   6| for generations.  In January, we hosted a community presentation.
   7| We started an Advisory program two years ago.  One goal of Advisory is to have students have at least one
   8| adult who will get to know him/her over the course of four years.  Students were randomly distributed into
   9| small grade-level groups.  They will stay with the same group of students, as well.   Another goal of Adviso-
  10| ry is to develop relationships with students of different backgrounds and social groups so that students can
  11| get to know each other and develop an appreciation of the diversity that we do have in Lunenburg.  Some
  12| Advisories will be facilitating some community service learning projects.

  14| The staff at Lunenburg High School focused their professional development activities in 2016-2017 working
  15| on curriculum development, curriculum mapping, District-Determined Measures, vertical teaming, and tech-
  16| nology.

===PAGE 122=== (plain, 51 lines)
   1| 122
   2| MONTACHUSETT REGIONAL TECHNICAL VOCATIONAL SCHOOL
   3| SUPERINTENDENT- DIRECTOR REPORT
   4| 2015-2016 was an exceptional year at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District.  We
   5| celebrated “50 years of Excellence in Education,” and through a thoughtful and reflective marketing campaign,
   6| we were able to honor the school’s history and highlight the successes of our proud alumni and notable
   7| achievements of our current students.  We launched a robust technology initiative, providing all teachers with
   8| technology and training in preparation for a whole-school Chromebook rollout in FY17.  Talented students,
   9| faculty and staff earned countless awards and recognitions, making our district so very proud.  And finally, the
  10| school began efforts to bring our 21st career and technical education program to the school – Veterinary
  11| Science.   This work would not be possible without the contributions of the many talented educators and
  12| administrators here at Monty Tech – a team I am honored to lead in my role as Superintendent-Director.  I am
  13| delighted to present the District’s 2015-2016 annual report to you, providing a snapshot of the wonderful
  14| experiences happening on a day-to-day basis here at Monty Tech, highlighting some of the year’s most notable
  15| achievements, including:
  16|  Efforts to bring the school’s 21 st vocational-technical program are well underway.  After a careful review
  17| of workforce projections and student interest surveys, school administrators have begun the process to
  18| bring a Chapter 74 Animal Science program to the district, which will be open to students Fall 2017.  This
  19| exciting STEM program will be housed in a state -of-the-art facility that boasts a classroom, science lab,
  20| grooming salon, and full -service veterinary clinic.  In an unprecedented fundraising campaign, school
  21| administrators have raised an impressive $1,975,461 in donations and in-kind matching services to support
  22| this project.  With an overall cost of $2,250,000, construction of the Monty Tech Veterinary Science
  23| Training Center and Community Clinic is in progress, providing tremendous han ds-on learning
  24| opportunities to students in our Carpentry, Plumbing, Electrical, Cabinetmaking, HVAC, Masonry, and
  25| Welding trades.
  26|  A unique partnership with Workers’ Credit Union has resulted in a full -service branch located on the
  27| school’s campus, and inc reased opportunities for students to develop authentic financial literacy skills
  28| while still in high school.  Students studying in the school’s Business Technology program are being
  29| trained as bank tellers, preparing them for entry -level jobs in banking in stitutions across North Central
  30| Massachusetts, and financial literacy workshops are being integrated into the curriculum each year.
  31|  Senior students in the school’s very popular Health Occupations program participated in the first year of
  32| an exciting new pa rtnership between Monty Tech and Mount Wachusett Community College.  This
  33| innovative high school-to-college collaboration, which embeds a comprehensive, college-level Emergency
  34| Medical Technician (EMT) training program into the senior year of study has res ulted in a number of
  35| students earning EMT credentials, providing additional career pathways in the medical field for these
  36| talented program graduates.
  37|  Students at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School continued to demonstrate high academic
  38| achievement.  In the spring of 2016, Monty Tech’s passing rate on the MCAS English Language Arts
  39| exam was 100%, Mathematics 99%, and Biology 99%.
  40| We are so proud of the educational programs offered here at Monty Tech, yet we continually strive to improve
  41| upon them.  Collaborating with area colleges and universities, we are ensuring our curriculum and instruction
  42| are rigorous and relevant.  Sharing best practices with vocational-technical colleagues from across the state –
  43| and nation – we give and take some of the best ideas, with one thing in mind – what is best for our students.
  44| And what is best, I believe, is preparing students for both college and career.
  45| We hope you will find this report a comprehensive review of the quality education you have come to know and
  46| expect from Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School.  You may notice that each of the eighteen
  47| member cities and towns are reflected in this report, and that students performed services in almost every
  48| community last year.  Providing our students with an opportunity to give back to the communities that support
  49| them – and support our school – is a pleasure.
  50|    Respectfully submitted,
  51| Sheila M. Harrity, Ed.D., Superintendent-Director

   2| Our Mission
   3| Every student will graduate from Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School with the skills,
   4| knowledge, and abilities to be a productive and effective member of an ever-changing society.
   5| Our District
   6| Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is a four -year career and tec hnical high school
   7| serving the member towns of:
   8| Ashburnham
   9| Ashby
  10| Athol
  11| Barre
  12| Fitchburg
  13| Gardner
  14| Harvard
  15| Holden
  16| Hubbardston
  17| Lunenburg
  18| Petersham
  19| Phillipston
  20| Princeton
  21| Royalston
  22| Sterling
  23| Templeton
  24| Westminster
  25| Winchendon
  26| Leadership
  27| The leadership team at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School is comprised of ten talented
  28| administrators whose varied educational backgrounds, professional experiences, and areas of expertise
  29| contribute to the success of the school. Working col laboratively, and under the direction of the
  30| Superintendent and Principal, the team has been able to transform the school into one of the most sought -
  31| after high schools in North Central Massachusetts.
  32| Sheila M. Harrity, Superintendent-Director
  33| Tom Browne, Principal
  34| Dayana Carlson, Assistant Principal
  35| Tammy Crockett, Business Manager
  36| Pamela Pothier, Director of Technology
  37| Christina Favreau, Director of Academic Programs
  38| Jim Hachey, Director of Vocational Programs
  39| Michael Gormley, Director of Facilities
  40| Katy Whitaker, Development Coordinator
  41| Victoria Zarozinski, Director of Student Support
  42| Services
  43| Enrollment
  44| On June 1,  2016, student enrollment at Monty Tech included  1,415 students in grades n ine through
  45| twelve. Students are represented from every community in the district: Ashburnham (56), Ashby (32),
  46| Athol (85), Barre (37), Fitchburg (362), Gardner (143), Harvard (4), Holden (60), Hubbardston (70),
  47| Lunenburg (86), Petersham (3), Phillipston (19), Princeton (22), Royalston (18), Sterling (63), Templeton
  48| (103), Westminster (77), and Winchendon (150). The remaining  25 students were from out -of-district
  49| towns, including Ayer, Clinton, Dudley, Groton, Leominster, Orange, and Worcester.
  50| Throughout 2015-2016, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and
  51| community members to learn about and visit the school.  In October 2015, approximately 700 district
  52| eighth graders participated in the  annual “Tour Day” event.   Students to ured our twenty vocational -
  53| technical areas and learned about the school’s challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and
  54| extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested students the opportunity to return in
  55| the evening with their family members to further explore the facilities and talk with staff members.
  56| Each year, the Dean of Admissions conducts school visits, student interviews, and accepts applications
  57| for admissions.  2015-2016 proved to be an exceptionally busy year for her, as the school received 631

===PAGE 124=== (plain, 44 lines)
   1| 124
   2| applications for admission.  Of those, 575 were from students hoping to enter our incoming freshman class.
   3| The balance of the applications came from students hoping to enter the school as upperclassmen.   Because
   4| there are only a limited number of students the school can accept each year, 338 freshmen and 3 upperclass-
   5| men were admitted.
   6| The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to visit
   7| Monty Tech after school and participate in hands -on learning experiences across a variety of vocational/
   8| technical areas. The program continued to attract a large number of students during the 2015 -2016 school
   9| year, serving approximately 600 area students.
  10| Class of 2016 Awards
  11| Members of the Class of 2016 were awarded approximately $58,000 in scholarships.  The Monty Tech Foun-
  12| dation generously provided $46,000 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts of $200 to
  13| $2,000.  The Foundation also awarded $4,000 to the Practical Nursing graduates.  Once again, local and state
  14| organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech
  15| graduates in the form of financial donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty and graduates
  16| themselves are grateful for this support.
  17| Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost of higher
  18| education.  Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through a number of articu-
  19| lation agreements with public and private colleges across the country.   Approximately 61% of the graduating
  20| class of 2016 reported plans to enroll at either a 2 -year college, a 4 -year college/university, or a technical/
  21| trade school upon graduation.  By earning college credits while still in high school, these students will benefit
  22| by saving both time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs.
  23| Financial Report
  24| In an effort to develop a cost -effective budget for the fiscal year 2015 -2016, a great deal of effort was put
  25| forth by the School Committee, administration, and staff.   The final fiscal year 2015 -2016 Educational Plan
  26| totaled $26,229,366, which represents a 2.3% increase over the 2014 -2015 Educational Plan.  The District’s
  27| FY16 budget only exceeds the minimum spending required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70 by
  28| $25,000 or .1%. The District was audited in October 2016 as part of the yearly financial audit by the account-
  29| ing firm of Melanson, Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA and a very good report is anticipated.
  30| Grants and Contracts
  31| Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis.  These funds help provide many education-
  32| al and social services to the student population.  For fiscal year 2016, state and federal grant sources provided
  33| the school with $918,426.  Programs funded by these grants include:  Essential Health Services, Social Inter-
  34| vention and Mediation, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement,
  35| Title I Support, Perkins Occupational Education, and Marine Corp Junior Reserve Officer Training.  The Dis-
  36| trict also received a competitive grant for $136,412 to purchase equipment for the anticipated new Veterinary
  37| Science Program. Using these allocation and competitive funds, the school was able to purchase a variety of
  38| instructional technology, equipment, and supplies to enhance the learning experience across both academic
  39| and vocational programs.
  40| Academic Achievement
  41| In 2015-2016, students at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School continued to demonstrate high
  42| academic achievement, earning commendable MCAS scores in English Language Arts, Mathematics and Bi-
  43| ology.  For spring 2016, Monty Tech’s passing rate on the English Language Arts was 100%, Mathematics
  44| 99%, Biology 99%.
