  21| The District continues to make progress toward narrowing proficiency gaps.  Students in all subgroups have
  22| met their progress and performance targets, securing the school’s Level 1 Status distinction.
  23| Vocational Projects in the District Communities
  24| Unlike students in traditional, comprehensive high schools, students at Monty Tech are asked to put their
  25| education into practice on a daily basis.  Students across the twenty different vocational-technical education
  26| programs are building homes, reconstructing damaged properties, repairing service vehicles, making walk-
  27| ways more accessible, and performing countless community services.  The 2015-2016 school year was a
  28| busy one for our vocational educators, as each trade aimed to provide practical, hands-on learning experi-
  29| ences for our students, while helping to improve each of the eighteen member communities.
  30| Auto Body Collision Repair Technology: The Monty Tech Auto Body program is led by a strong instruc-
  31| tional team, always eager to enhance the program to benefit the students.  This year instructor Michael
  32| Forhan revised the freshman curriculum to expand the detailing component, which has been met with great
  33| enthusiasm from students and clients alike.  Freshmen and sophomores continue to earn industry recog-
  34| nized credentials, including the I-CAR Pro level one certification in refinishing and non-structural damage.
  35| This certification not only makes our program graduates more employable, it enables students to maintain
  36| the I-CAR gold class certification.  Throughout the year, students detailed, repaired, and repainted vehicles,
  37| fulfilling more than seventy requests that included:  an SUV police vehicle car for the Town of Winchen-
  38| don, a wooden statue of Johnny Appleseed, and the antique playground fire truck for the Westminster Lions
  39| Club.

   3| operative education placements, demonstrating their technical skills in the workplace, and all senior students
   4| received an Environmental Protection Agency certificate.  (Total enrollment: 62; 35 males, 27 females)
   5| Automotive Technology:  As in past years, the Automotive Technology program continues to service a variety
   6| of vehicles for faculty, staff, municipalities, and district residents.  2015-2016 proved to be a very busy year for
   7| the program, as more than four hundred vehicles were serviced by talented students and instructors.  In addition
   8| to maintaining the school’s fleet of vehicles throughout the year, students were asked to repair a Salvation Army
   9| disaster relief vehicle, and it was a pleasure to give back to this deserving organization.  Students performed
  10| very well in the SkillsUSA state competitions, placing third, fourth, and fifth, with hopes to improve in the com-
  11| ing year.  A total of five students participated in the Co-Op Program, applying their technical skills in real work
  12| settings. Finally, instructors are proud to announce three talented program graduates are working in shops in the
  13| area, demonstrating the skills they acquired at Monty Tech.  (Total enrollment: 62; 50 males, 12 females)
  14| Business Technology:  Students in Monty Tech’s Business technology program are seeing tremendous benefits
  15| to the new partnership with Workers’ Credit Union.  The opportunity to participate in teller training and finan-
  16| cial literacy workshops has been met with great enthusiasm from our students, and the instructors continue to be
  17| grateful for this developing partnership.  Four students were offered co-op placements with WCU during the
  18| 2015-2016 school year as bank tellers, and the two Seniors were offered employment upon graduation.  Monty
  19| Tech Business Technology instructors, in collaboration with their post-secondary colleagues, have developed
  20| two articulation agreements, with Mount Wachusett Community College and New England Institute of Technol-
  21| ogy, which will provide qualified students with college credits at no cost to the student.  Opportunities to
  22| demonstrate customer service, cash handling, and accounting skills are ever-present as the Business technology
  23| students successfully operate an in-house retail location and greenhouse.  The program’s instructional technolo-
  24| gy was updated to allow students to test on the most up-to-date version of Microsoft for their certifications.

  37| could build upon the strong technical foundation they have attained at Monty Tech.   (Total enrollment: 73; 50
  38| males, 23 females)
  39| Cosmetology:  Monty Tech is pleased to introduce Emily Bedard, a talented new instructor in the school’s popu-

   6| Program Advisory Committee dinner meetings, four Monty Tech Foundation breakfasts, Monty Tech’s Retirees
   7| holiday luncheon, a graduation reception, the Women in Technology event, Principal and Counselors Day, the
   8| Monty Tech homecoming dance, MAVA meetings and all School Committee meetings and sub-committee

  11| also participated in the Montachusett Opportunity Council’s “Taste of North Central” fundraiser.  The pro-

  13| worked side-by-side with some of the area’s finest chefs, preparing a six-course dinner with extensive hors
  14| d’oeuvres for 380 guests.  (Total enrollment: 96; 34 males, 62 females)
  15| Dental Assisting:  During 2015-2016, the Dental Assisting program introduced nineteen students to industry
  16| experience through externships, while ten students participated in affiliation, and one student earned a co-
  17| operative educational placement, working with an area dentist. All sophomore, junior, and senior students at-
  18| tended the Yankee Dental Convention in Boston, and learned about the most current trends and practices in the
  19| field.  While the national pass rate for the Dental Assisting National Board (DANB) Infection Control Exam
  20| was 86% in FY15, the Monty Tech students far surpassed that rate with a 100% passing rate on the Infection
  21| Control exam.  For the sixth consecutive year, Monty Tech welcomed Community Health Connections, a
  22| school-based dental hygiene program, whose goal is to provide dental services to students in need.  Through
  23| this initiative, more than thirty students were examined by a dentist, had their teeth cleaned, and had sealants or
  24| temporary fillings applied as needed.  Monty Tech Dental Assisting students were given hands-on, practical
  25| experience, as they assisted the staff from CHC during each dental procedure that was performed. (Total enroll-
  26| ment: 58; 5 males, 53 females)
  27| Drafting:  The 2015-2016 school year was a busy one for Drafting Technology students and instructors. A total
  28| of six students (one junior and five seniors) participated in the school’s Co-Op Program, and four students ad-
  29| vanced to the SkillsUSA state competitions.  Instructors are pleased to report that a majority of the graduating

  36| and installed signage throughout the building for the 2016 graduation, College Fair, School of Continuing Educa-
  37| tion, sports and drama clubs.  (Total enrollment: 57; 36 males, 21 females)
  38| Early Childhood Education:  The Early Childhood Education program received three new Baby Care Parent
  39| Simulation dolls, each with car seat detections and temperature detectors.  This added technology will assist

   2| Electrical:  The Electrical program continues to be one of the busiest trades in the school.  In 2015-2016, more
   3| than fifty work orders were completed throughout the building.  The wiring of various equipment included: a
   4| new media blaster in welding, a television in cosmetology, replacing the fixture in the elevator, re-wiring the
   5| HVAC shop, wiring the new air conditioner in the Technology Department, and adding receptacles in the sci-
   6| ence room for new labs.  In addition to wiring equipment, Electrical students performed numerous repairs of
   7| lights, outlets, computers, sensors, CATV jacks, and new data drops for computers.  In addition to participat-
   8| ing in the house building project in Ashburnham, upperclassmen students rewired a guard shack at the West-
   9| minster Crocker Pond, and the electrical shop motor lab was remodeled to incorporate twenty-four booths.
  10| Fifteen students earned co-op positions, and instructors are proud to announce that 60% of the graduating
  11| class entered electrical apprenticeships. (Total enrollment: 83; 69 males, 14 females)
  12| Engineering Technology:  The Monty Tech Engineering Technology program continues to further enhance
  13| the curriculum and instruction by improving upon the four Project Lead the Way course modules, and adding
  14| more Computer Integrated Manufacturing content into the already rigorous vocational-technical curriculum.
  15| Field Programmable Gate Array mini systems were purchased and integrated into the Digital Electronics cur-
  16| riculum. Engineering students performed well in SkillsUSA competitions, earning a number of top awards.
  17| At SkillsUSA at Districts, Monty Tech Engineering students received two gold, four silver, and four bronze
  18| medals.  At SkillsUSA States, students earned two gold and one silver medal, and finally two top students
  19| traveled to Louisville, KY to compete at the National level in Mechatronics.  The Freshman Exploratory Pro-
  20| gram yielded positive results for the program, with eleven first choice freshmen.  The majority of the graduat-
  21| ing class will continue their education in the engineering field. (Total enrollment: 48; 38 males, 10 females)
  22| Graphic Communications:  The Monty Tech Graphic Communications program is pleased to report that all
  23| graduating seniors were accepted to area colleges and universities.  The program benefited from twenty-four
  24| new iMac Computers with Adobe CC software, and our lab was renovated to accommodate this wonderful
  25| new technology.  Throughout the 2015-2016 school year, the shop continued to produce large quantities of
  26| print projects for district towns and community service organizations.  This year, over three hundred print
  27| projects were completed, saving local organizations approximately $145,000.  Twenty-two freshmen chose
  28| Graphic Communications as their top program, and two additional students joined our program, resulting in a
  29| freshman class of 24 students.  The future looks bright for the Monty Tech Graphic Communications pro-
  30| gram. Three students earned co-op placements.  Our students continue to perform well in related SkillsUSA
  31| competitions, and earned four medals at the District event - one silver and one gold in Advertising and De-
  32| sign, one gold in Screenprinting, and one bronze medal in Digital Cinema Production.  (Total enrollment: 90;
  33| 37 males, 53 females)
  34| Health Occupations:  The Health Occupations program at Monty Tech continues to educate a large number of
  35| students, providing a rigorous education grounded in current medical knowledge and practice. 2015-2016 was
  36| an exciting year for the program, as a new partnership with Mount Wachusett Community College enabled
  37| senior students to participate in an Emergency Medical Technician training program, earning eight college
  38| credits at no cost.  The program is dedicated to providing all students with opportunities to earn industry-
  39| recognized credentials, and as a result, students also earn the following credentials:  Occupational Safety and
  40| Health Administration (OSHA) 10-hour certification; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (CPR) and First Aid
  41| Certificate through the American Heart Association; and Certified Nursing Assistant License through the
  42| American Red Cross.  This year, 94% of the senior students passed the National Healthcare Association
  43| (CCMA) exam. To support the students’ desire to give back, a “Baby Shower” benefitting Battered Women's
  44| Resources, Inc. was held; students participated in Pediatric Day with the Early Childhood Education program
  45| and also raised $700 for Special Olympics. (Total enrollment: 106; 10 males, 96 females)
  46| House Carpentry:  Most of the work done by the students and instructors in the Monty Tech House Carpentry
  47| program is done off-campus, completing renovations, buildings, and repair work for member communities.
  48| Some of the projects completed during the 2015-2016 school year include:  building a two-story colonial
  49| home in Ashburnham for Montachusett Enterprise Center, Inc. (MEC), building two decks for a Habitat for

   2| Humanity house project in Fitchburg, building a shed for the Monty Tech football team, building a storage

   9| profit organizations throughout the district were also built by the talented Carpentry students at Monty Tech.
  10| (Total enrollment: 68; 50 males, 18 females)
  11| HVAC & Property Maintenance:  The 2015-2016 school year was the first full year for the newly enhanced
  12| HVAC & Property Maintenance program, which is now aligned to the state frameworks more accurately.
  13| The program’s new name and added curriculum is expected to present our students with additional career
  14| pathways and opportunities to participate in a co-operative work environment.  Employers are discovering
  15| that we have more to offer, and students are finding themselves with more employable skill sets. As the
  16| shop continues its enhancements to the HVAC portion of our program, we are now fully involved with six
  17| student work modules, as well as a full-size commercial air chiller unit.  New tooling to accommodate these
  18| changes includes the acquisition of six new lockable storage cabinet workbench workstations.  These sta-
  19| tions are outfitted with the necessary tooling used in the refrigeration trade, complete with test meters and
  20| manifold gauge sets.  Nine Seniors and six Juniors participated in the very popular co-operative education
  21| program. (Total enrollment: 64; 56 males, 8 females)
  22| Information Technology:  As with any school, Information Technology provides key services to the educa-
  23| tional community.  In addition to the critical in-house Help Desk services offered by the program, students
  24| and instructors performed in excess of 400 hours of repair, upgrading, and troubleshooting computer prob-
  25| lems for Monty Tech community members.  Some of our students compete in the Cyber Patriot event, a na-
  26| tional high school cyber defense competition, founded by the Air Force Association. A team of eight stu-

  29| General Safety Certification.  One senior and one junior developed websites for non-profit organizations -

  31| SkillsUSA competitions.  In the District competition, two seniors were awarded gold medals in the 3D visu-
  32| alization & animation competition, two juniors earned the gold in the Information Technology Services
  33| competition, and two sophomores brought home gold and silver medals in Internetworking.  At the state
  34| level, two talented juniors earned Silver medals in Internetworking. (Total enrollment: 63; 58 males, 5 fe-
  35| males)
  36| Machine Technology:   The Monty Tech Machine Technology Program continues to prepare students to
  37| achieve Level 1 and Level 2 MACWIC (Manufacturing Advancement Center for Workforce Innovation
  38| Collaborative) certification.  Through a beneficial relationship with the Massachusetts Manufacturing Exten-
  39| sion Partnership, our instructors have access to high quality curriculum developed at WPI.  Articulation
  40| agreements with Mount Wachusett Community College and Quinsigamond Community College were devel-

  46| enrollment: 58; 57 males, 1 female)

   7| Student Support Services
   8| During the 2015-2016 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided
   9| special services to approximately three hundred and fifteen students – measuring progress of over two hun-
  10| dred students on Individual Education Plans (IEPs) and just under one hundred students adhering to individ-
  11| ualized Section 504 plans.  While the Student Support Services (SSS) Department encompasses special edu-
  12| cation, the department provides support and is available to all Monty Tech students.
  13| The department includes a full-time nursing staff that administers medications, performs state-mandated
  14| health screening exams, and provides, when necessary, health information to the special education team for
  15| a student’s IEP meeting.  The department benefits from a full-time school social worker who participates in
  16| departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning finances, family issues, homeless-
  17| ness, maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing. The school is also fortunate to have on staff a
  18| full-time psychologist, whose role it is to evaluate all students referred for an initial evaluation or who re-
  19| quire a three-year re-evaluation. In addition, we have a full-time speech language pathologist, who is availa-
  20| ble to assist students with disabilities, assess these students and consult with teachers.  Our students also
  21| have access to the services of a full-time adjustment counselor and part-time school psychologist. All of
  22| these individuals are available for scheduled counseling sessions and mental health emergency treatment, as
  23| well as crisis intervention.
  24| The school’s Director of Student Support Services oversees the District’s Special Education Program, which
  25| is reviewed annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements. The comprehensive review and
  26| evaluation are done in collaboration with the Parent Advisory Council, and the results of the evaluation are
  27| used to improve the special education procedures and programs in place at Montachusett Regional Vocation-
  28| al Technical School.
  29| Technology
  30| In 2015-2016, the Monty Tech Technology Department completed a three-year technology plan and review,
  31| in preparation for a whole-school Chromebook roll-out in FY17.  Training efforts continued throughout the
  32| year, to ensure teacher capacity in the Google for Education platform.
  33| A sophisticated Liebert air conditioning system was installed in the Main Distribution Facility (MDF) to
  34| maintain constant temperature and humidity supporting the growing demands of the MDF.
  35| Three Chromebook mobile labs were added to the fleet of mobile computer labs that circulate among class-
  36| rooms providing the students with modern technology.  A self-service LobbyGuard kiosk system was imple-
  37| mented that manages visitors to the building and increases school safety measures.  The cafeteria received an
  38| upgrade to their POS system, utilizing advanced technology in school nutrition and meal planning.  Candi-
  39| dates for free and reduced lunch may now complete an application online, managing payments and fees us-
  40| ing this new online system.  Finally, in preparation of the anticipated 2016-2017 Chromebook roll-out, the
  41| technology staff was reorganized. Duties were reallocated among staff and leadership, transitioning to a
  42| more efficient department.  New systems and policies are continually being researched to support this initia-
  43| tive.

   2| SkillsUSA
   3| SkillsUSA is a partnership of students, teachers and industry working together to ensure America has a skilled
   4| workforce.  Through our association with SkillsUSA, our students develop job skills, leadership and profes-
   5| sional skills, as well as provide community service.  The 2015-2016 school year was an extraordinary one for
   6| our chapter of SkillsUSA. The students met the challenges of districts, performed well at states and prepared
   7| for the National Conference. They also conducted several community service projects and raised money for
   8| various deserving charities.  Serving as co-advisors were Kelsey Moskiwitz, English Instructor, Anne Marie
   9| Cataldo, Early Childhood Education Instructor, and Brad Pelletier, Special Education Instructor.  Highlights of
  10| the year include:
  11| In September 2015, forty-one students applied and were accepted to form the Monty Tech SkillsUSA Leader-
  12| ship Team. After two leadership training sessions, seven officers were elected.
  13| In November 2015, sixteen students, consisting of chapter officers and Leadership Team members attended the
  14| Annual Fall State Leadership Conference, where they participated in workshops and leadership exercises
  15| and performed community service at an area YMCA Day Camp.
  16| The SkillsUSA local competitions took place November 2015, and on December 18th, the students learned who
  17| would advance to the next round of competition.
  18| A total of forty-seven medals were captured at the District Competition held in March 2016 at Bay Path Re-
  19| gional Vocational Technical School:  fourteen gold, sixteen silver and seventeen bronze medals.  Outstand-
  20| ing student Grace Kirrane qualified to run for the State Executive Council, as well.
  21| Olivia Houle, a junior in the Welding program, was selected to serve on the State Advisory Committee to help
  22| aid in the planning of the State Conference.
  23| Thirty-four district medalists and qualifiers, nine local leadership and occupational related event contestants,
  24| one state officer candidate and nineteen voting delegates for a total of sixty-three students attended the
  25| State Leadership and Skills Conference, held in April 2016 at Blackstone Valley Vocational Technical
  26| School. There, seven students were awarded gold medals, and earned the right to compete at the very com-
  27| petitive National Leadership & Skills Conference held in late June.
  28| Nine students and six instructors attended the National Leadership & Skills Conference in Louisville, KY in
  29| June 2016.  There, Taylor Sadowski, a graduate from the Health Occupations program, earned a silver
  30| medal in the Medical Assisting contest.
  31| In August 2016, Grace Kirrane attended the SkillsUSA Massachusetts state leadership training where she was
  32| elected to serve as the SkillsUSA Massachusetts State Vice President.
  33| Marine Corps JROTC
  34| The Monty Tech Marine Corps Junior Reserve Officer Training Corps (MCJROTC) had a sensational school
  35| year.  All program objectives for the 2015-2016 school year were achieved, most notable was the JROTC
  36| Cyber Security Team, led by First Sergeant Paul Jornet and Information Technology Instructor Richard Dun-
  37| can, once again capturing national recognition by placing fourth overall in the Air Force Association’s Nation-
  38| al Cyber Security Competition held in Baltimore, MD.

===PAGE 133=== (layout, 41 lines)
   1| The 2015-2016 Corps of Cadets completed over eighty-eight hundred hours of community service through-
   2| out the district. The corps conducted a major canned food drive, worked side-by-side with the Marine Corps
   3| Reserve in a national Toys 4 Tots campaign, and spent five weekends working with the local Salvation Army
   4| helping to raise over $25,000 for needy families.  The cadets conducted their annual 10 mile “March-A-
   5| Thon” to support NEADS (National Education of Assistance Dogs Services) from Princeton, MA, raising
   6| $20,000 for their Service Dogs for Veterans program.
   7| Seventy Monty Tech cadets attended a leadership camp at Prince William Forest, in Quantico VA.  The camp
   8| provided cadets with individual and team development opportunities, as well as the opportunity to visit our
   9| nation’s capital and many other historical sites.
  10| During the summer of 2016, our Cadet Cyber Team was again asked to support a Cyber STEM camp for the
  11| National Marine Corps JROTC program. The Cyber Team coordinated the 8-day camp that focused on de-
  12| fending cyber networks from attacks. The camp also offered an introduction to robotics programming using
  13| the VEX Robotics System.  The camp was attended by 200 students, representing twenty-two states.
  14|  The Monty Tech JROTC program once again was awarded the designation of Marine Corps Honor School
  15| by the Commanding General Marine Corps Training & Education Command.  Only ten percent of Marine
  16| Corps JROTC programs nationwide receive this recognition.  The selection was based on several criteria,
  17| including cadet citizenship, the percentage of cadets involved in student government organizations, the num-
  18| ber of cadets active in school activities and clubs, the number of community service hours for the unit as a
  19| whole and per cadet, the number of organizations assisted by the unit, and scholastic achievements including
  20| the number of academic awards and scholarships given to cadets.  The units were also measured by participa-
  21| tion in public affairs events, drill team competitions, rifle team competitions, physical fitness team competi-
  22| tions, and field trips.
  23| Because of this prestigious status, Monty Tech’s Senior Marine Instructor has the authority   to make nomi-
  24| nation recommendations to the U.S. Naval Academy, U.S. Air Force Academy, and the Military Academy at
  25| West Point under the Honor School Category.
  26| Women in Technology
  27| Monty Tech is one of the original members of the North Central Massachusetts Women In Technology Pro-th
  28| gram, and the 2015-2016 school year marked the school’s 14                   year in this innovative school-to-business
  29| partnership.  The highly successful collaborative program continues to grow with membership comprised of
  30| students from six area high schools. Its mission is to encourage female students to explore well-paying ca-
  31| reers in the fields of business and technology.  Participants spend two days a month, working on real-world
  32| work projects under the mentorship of company managers at SimplexGrinnell and Tyco Safety Products in
  33| Westminster, subsidiaries of Tyco International, a Fortune 500 company.
  34| The program has been so successful in its mission of providing a proven pathway to corporate America, that
  35| it was recently recognized as one of the region’s premier experience-based educational programs of its kind.
  36| Affiliation with the program, and skills acquired through participation, open doors to career opportunities not
  37| otherwise available to high school students. Graduates of the program are also equipped with a foundation to
  38| better meet the challenges of an ever-changing and demanding work force.
  39| Each year, graduates of the program go on to rewarding and well-paying careers, made possible by this
  40| unique experience.

  15| robust reporting features and a very intuitive student and instructor portal.  This web-based software

  22| meeting the needs of the community.  We are in line to accomplish both!  Our goal in the coming year
  23| includes increasing our adult education course offerings, with a concentration on career programs.  Stay
  24| tuned for Pharmacy Technician, Medical Billing and Coding, Auto Damage Appraiser and
  25| Apprenticeship opportunities at the Monty Tech School of Continuing Education.
  26| Practical Nursing Program
  27| The Practical Nursing Program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely, ethically and in a
  28| caring manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in health status in diverse health care
  29| settings.  This mission which is consistent with the philosophy and goals of the Montachusett Regional
  30| Vocational Technical School District accomplishes the following:
  31| · Identifies a strong relationship between academic and vocational preparation
  32| · Stresses the importance of developing critical thinking skills to function safely, effectively, and
  33| productively in an ever-changing technical and diverse society
  34| · Supports the maintenance of a positive and caring learning and practice environment
  35| The above mission and philosophy were met by the Practical Nursing program by several different
  36| methods.
  37| On June 23, 2016, a graduating class of 32 students completed the Practical Nursing Program and entered
  38| the nursing profession.  The class achieved an initial NCLEX pass rate of 91%, with 29 of the 32
  39| graduates achieving 100% pass rate for the NCLEX-PN exam. Three graduates are waiting to sit their
  40| NCLEX-PN exam.  94% of the 2016 graduates are currently employed in the health care profession
  41| throughout Massachusetts, and many are working within the eighteen cities and towns of the Monty Tech
  42| school district as LPNs in various health care settings, ranging from long term care, sub-acute care to
  43| mental health facilities.

===PAGE 136=== (plain, 42 lines)
   1| 136
   2| The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program continues to develop the “LPN to BSN Bridge” relationship
   3| with Fitchburg State University.  Several 2016 graduates are pursuing seats in the LPN to BSN program at
   4| FSU and will be continuing their education to the Bachelors in Nursing. Many present class applicants cited
   5| as their attraction to Monty Tech as the opportunity to complete a ten-month accelerated program and then
   6| bridge to Fitchburg State University.
   7| Monty Tech Practical Nursing students are now completing patient scenarios in the Sim Lab on a weekly
   8| basis in Term 2 and Term 3. The Faculty Sim team has implemented National League of Nursing (NLN)
   9| patient scenarios consistent with our curriculum frameworks and have also utilized the Sim lab setting to
  10| instruct students in developing nursing skills that they may not be experiencing in the clinical setting.  In-
  11| structors have successfully developed a pediatric patient scenario that is consistent with present student
  12| learning outcomes and the clinical pediatric setting.
  13| The Practical Nursing Program Faculty implemented new student learning outcomes across the curriculum
  14| adopted from the Massachusetts Board of Higher Education Nurse of the Future Initiative LPN Competen-
  15| cies. The competencies have been recognized nationally as being consistent with current LPN practice. The
  16| Faculty will assess the revised student learning outcomes and our content to assure consistency with the new
  17| NCLEX-PN Detailed Test Plan that will be released in March 2017.
  18| The class of 2016 performed a community service project adopting a Monty Tech family in need referred by
  19| Student Support Services. The nursing students purchased holiday gifts and food for the family, making sure
  20| they were consistent with the family’s requests or needs.
  21| With an eye toward the program’s future, forty-seven applicants were accepted to the Class of 2017, and
  22| forty-five students enrolled and will continue in the tradition of high-quality practical nursing preparation.
  23| Looking Ahead
  24| While the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District educational community is certainly
  25| proud of the achievements of our talented students, faculty, and staff, we continue to have an eye toward the
  26| future, always committed to improving our vocational and academic programming, strengthening key part-
  27| nerships, and maintaining facilities that contribute to student success and achievement.  As we look ahead,
  28| there are a number of programs and initiatives that we expect will have a positive impact on our school and
  29| students for years to come.
  30| Expand partnerships with area businesses:  The Machine Technology program at Monty Tech has a long
  31| history of successfully placing students in machine and manufacturing shops in the area.  Program instruc-
  32| tors work closely with a nineteen-member program advisory committee to ensure instruction and training
  33| equipment aligns with current industry standards, and welcome their guidance in this regard.  With an eye
  34| toward expanding opportunities for students interested in pursuing occupations in this high-demand indus-
  35| try, program partner L. S. Starrett Company has suggested adding lessons in quality control and metrology
  36| to the existing curriculum.  Program instructors anticipate reconfiguring the instructional space to bring in
  37| updated equipment, and will also add two industry-recognized certification exams to the curriculum.  By
  38| standardizing metrology instruction and providing students with opportunities to earn additional credentials,
  39| program instructors are ensuring Monty Tech Machine Technology students are poised for a successful
  40| school-to-work transition upon graduation.
  41| Expand partnerships with area colleges and universities:  Monty Tech students currently benefit from a
  42| number of articulation and dual enrollment agreements with surrounding post-secondary partners.

===PAGE 137=== (plain, 27 lines)
   1| 137
   2| In the coming years, we hope to expand those opportunities in two vocational -technical areas:  Information
   3| Technology and Early Childhood Education.  By the end of 2016 -2017, Monty Tech Information Technolo-
   4| gy instructors will submit an articulation request to MWCC officials, to determine how our curriculum may
   5| align with MWCC’s Computer Information Systems Transfer Track (CIT) program. Additionally, instructors
   6| in Monty Tech’s Early Childhood Education program will continue discussions with Fitchburg State Univer-
   7| sity officials, exploring the potential of embedding one college -level course into the senior year curriculum.
   8| School officials continue to discuss the merits of aligning curriculum, securing appropriate adjunct professor
   9| credentials, and costs associated with tuition and fees for the agreed upon course.
  10| Expand Access to Instructional Technology:  A Monty Tech education is effective because efforts are made
  11| to continually assess our programming, instructional equipment, and our educational resources.  Through
  12| this assessment, it has become clear that while so many students have access to technology away from the
  13| school, many do not. It is important, then, to arm every student with the tools necessary to succeed not only
  14| in the workplace, but in the classroom, as well.  The district is pleased to announce a whole-school technolo-
  15| gy initiative, culminating in a September 2016 Chromebook distribution to each of our 1,435 students.  It is
  16| our hope that these devices will support learning, increase and streamline communication with teachers, and
  17| promote collaboration among student peers.
  18| Expand vocational-technical educational opportunities :  As you know, Montachusett Regional Vocational
  19| Technical School is home to twenty vocational-technical programs, and currently serves over 1,400 students
  20| from eighteen cities and towns in North Central Massachusetts.  We are so proud of our students and accom-
  21| plishments, and every year we strive to provide only the best educational programs, both academic and voca-
  22| tional, to each and every student.  As we review our student demographic, the regional workforce data, and
  23| feedback we have received from students interested in attending our school, we consider the benefits of add-
  24| ing new vocational programs.  We are pleased to announce that Monty Tech is bringing our 21 st program to
  25| the school – Animal/Veterinary Science.  The new program, open to students Fall 2017, will respond to
  26| much-needed workforce training and community services, and will effectively prepare students to enter both
  27| college and career pathways upon graduation.

   1|                                                   TOWN MEETING EXCERPTS
   2|                                                           Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   3| The Annual Town Meeting was called to order at 9:05 A.M. in the Lunenburg High School Au-
   4| ditorium (it was noted that this is the last town meeting to be held in the Cushing Auditorium
   5| as the new Middle/High School will be completed soon) a quorum was declared present and the
   6| warrant noted as being properly served. We pledged allegiance, the Moderator went over the
   7| rules of conduct and also noted a conflict with Article 45 relative to the Water District (he is
   8| Moderator for the District) and would appoint Tom Bodkin to serve as Moderator for that ar-
   9| ticle.
  10| ARTICLE 1.       To see if the Town will vote to re-authorize in accordance with MGL Chapter 44,
  11| §53E½, the Ambulance Billing Revolving Fund.  The purpose of this fund will be to cover the cost
  12| associated with compensating third party advanced life support companies and third party billing
  13| companies that contract and supply services to the Town.  Five percent (5%) of the total revenue gen-
  14| erated by ambulance services rendered and 100% of the revenue generated by Advanced Life Sup-
  15| port services rendered shall be deposited into said revolving fund.  The proceeds of said Revolving
  16| Fund shall be expended by the Fire Department without further appropriation for the sole purpose of
  17| reimbursing the aforementioned companies for services rendered to the Town; expenditures from the
  18| revolving fund may not exceed $65,000 during the fiscal year without the approval of the Board of
  19| Selectmen and Finance Committee or take any other action relative thereto. (Submitted by Fire
  20| Chief)  Board of Selectmen recommends approval. Finance Committee recommends approval. VOT-
  21| ED UNANIMOUS CONSENT
  22| ARTICLE 2.        To see if the Town will vote to re-authorize in accordance with MGL Chapter 44,
  23| §53E½, the Timber Rights Revolving Fund, such fund to be redited with the proceeds of sale by the
  24| Conservation Commission of the rights to harvest timber; funds in the account to be expended for the
  25| acquisition of land for conservation purposes and associated costs of such acquisitions, including but
  26| not limited to appraisals and payment of accrued taxes; such funds may also be expended for im-
  27| provements on land already under the custody and control of the Conservation Commission; the Con-
  28| servation Commission may expend such funds without further appropriation; expenditures from the
  29| revolving fund may not exceed $10,000 during the fiscal year without the approval of the Board of
  30| Selectmen and Finance Committee; or take any other action relative thereto. (Submitted by Conser-
  31| vation Commission)  Board of Selectmen recommends approval. Finance Committee recommends
  32| approval. VOTED UNANIMOUS CONSENT
  33| ARTICLE 3.        To see if the Town will vote to re-authorize in accordance with MGL Chapter 44,
  34| §53E½, the Custodial Special Detail Revolving Fund, for the purpose of paying special detail salaries
  35| for outside functions.  Charges and money received from outside organizations in connection with
  36| this program are to be deposited in said revolving fund and expended by the Lunenburg School Com-
  37| mittee without further appropriation for the sole purpose of paying personnel for hours worked.  Said
  38| revolving account expenditures shall not exceed $13,000 per year without the approval of the Board
  39| of Selectmen and Finance Committee; or take any other action relative thereto. (Submitted by School
  40| Committee) Board of Selectmen recommends approval. Finance Committee recommends approval.
  41| VOTED UNANIMOUS CONSENT

   3| ARTICLE 39. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Zoning Bylaw, Section 6.0, General Pro-
   4| visions Affecting All Districts, by deleting Sections 6.1.2 Design of Off Street Parking and Loading
   5| Spaces and 6.4.3 Design Requirements and adding a new Section 6.4.3 Off Street Parking Design
   6| Standards, or take any action relative thereto. (Submitted by Planning Board) Board of Selectmen
   7| and Finance Committee will make recommendation at town meeting.
   8| 6.4.3. Off-Street Parking Design Standards
   9| General Parking & Loading Provisions
  10| Location
  11|  1. Required off -street parking spaces shall be provided on the same lot as, and loading bays
  12| shall be provided next to, the principal or accessory use they are required to serve.
  13|  2. No area may be utilized and counted as both a required parking space and a required loading
  14| bay, without the approval of the Planning Board. Shared parking/loading areas shall only be
  15| used for loading prior to opening or after closing of all uses located on the site in question.
  16| However, maneuvering aisles and driveways may serve both required parking and loading bays
  17| if they meet the design standards of each. Existing areas used for both parking and loading shall
  18| be counted for loading purposes.
  19|  3. Required off -street parking spaces or loading bays may be wholly or partly enclosed in a
  20| structure.
  21|  4. Off-street parking spaces required for two or more buildings, uses, or establishments on a
  22| single lot may be provided in a common lot.
  23| Driveways
  24|  1. Each parking space and loading bay shall be connected by a driveway to a street or to an inte-
  25| rior drive that leads to a street.
  26|  2. The number of driveways permitting entrance to and exit from a lot shall be limited to two
  27| per street line. Driveways shall be located to minimize conflict with traffic on public streets and
  28| where good visibility and sight distances are available to observe approaching pedestrian and
  29| vehicular traffic.
  30|  3. All curb cuts and access driveways shall be located in the Zoning District in which the site’s
  31| primary use is permitted.
  32|  4. The maximum grade of any outdoor driveway is 12%.
  33|  5. No Driveway shall require that vehicles back out onto public or private ways.
  34| Setbacks
  35|  1. Each parking space or driveway serving a one-family or two-family dwelling shall be set
  36| back five feet from any side lot line and rear lot line, with the exception of common driveways

===PAGE 158=== (layout, 33 lines)
   1|                                      Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   2| and shall be designated on a plan and is issued a building permit by the Building Commissioner.
   3|     2. No parking is permitted in the front yard other than in a designated parking space or drive-
   4|     way.
   5|     3. All paved parts of all parking spaces, driveways and maneuvering aisles shall be set back
   6|     from any wall of a principal building and from any lot line or zoning boundary line as indicated
   7|     in the following table:
   8|        District            Residential Dis-            Street Line             All Other Lot          Wall of Principal
   9|                                trict Line                                           Lines                  Building
  10|  RA; RB; O; R,                    N/A                        25                        5                        5
  11|  VCD
  12|  LB/R;                             25                        25                       10                        5
  13|  RC; C                             40                        30                       15                        5
  14|  OP/I                              50                        30                                                 5
  15|     *all measurements are in feet
  16| Exceptions to the Table Are:
  17|    a. One and Two-Family homes
  18|         b. A parking space located within a structure otherwise permitted in such area
  19|     4. No parking space, internal driveway or loading bay, whether required or otherwise provided,
  20|     shall be located, wholly or partly, within the right-of-way of a street.
  21|     5. All access to parking shall be by driveways meeting the requirements of this Section; curbs,
  22|     wheel stops, screening or similar barriers must be installed to prevent vehicles from being
  23|     parked or driven within required setback areas.
  24|     Screening for Parking
  25|     1. In all residential and mixed uses, or on a lot in any district which abuts or is across the street
  26|     from a residential use, any outdoor parking lot, all loading bays, maneuvering aisles and drive-
  27|     ways shall be screened in a manner to protect abutting lots from the glare of headlights, noise
  28|     and other nuisance factors.
  29|     2. Any parking lot, which is a principal use, must be screened along driveways and around the
  30|     entire perimeter of the parking lot. The entrance to driveways, to the extent practicable, shall be
  31|     located on the side near nonresidential uses or on streets or highways leading to nonresidential
  32|     areas.
  33|                                                             158

===PAGE 159=== (plain, 41 lines)
   1| 159
   2| Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   3| 3. Screening shall consist of:
   4| a. A strip of land at least four feet wide, densely planted with combination of shrubs, and
   5| trees with or without earthen berms which shall have a combined height of at least four feet
   6| high at the time of planting and which are of a type that may be expected to form a year-
   7| round dense screen at least six feet high within three years;
   8| b. A wall, barrier, or fence of uniform appearance at least five feet high above finished
   9| grade. Such wall, barrier or fence may be opaque or perforated, provided that not more than
  10| 50% of the face is open; or
  11| c. Any combination of a & b approved by the Planning Board through Site Plan Approval.
  12| 4. Such screening shall be maintained in good condition at all times. Such screening or barrier
  13| may be interrupted by entrances or exits and shall have no signs attached thereto other than
  14| those permitted in the district.
  15| Design Standards
  16| Dimensions
  17| a. Parking spaces and maneuvering aisles shall have the minimum dimensions set forth in the
  18| following table and elsewhere in this section:
  19| Table 1
  20| S = Standard Parking Space    C= Compact Parking Space
  21| NOTES:

  23| the width shall be 12 feet.

  25| no obstructions to the vehicles overhang.

===PAGE 160=== (plain, 39 lines)
   1| 160
   2| Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   3| b. To count as a required parking space, a parallel parking space shall have maneuvering
   4| space at least 20 feet deep in front of it in an aisle parallel to and abutting such parking space.
   5| c. Where columns of a building or structure are located in a parking lot (such as a parking
   6| garage under a building) no part of a column may be within three feet of a maneuvering aisle
   7| or within the minimum dimensions of a parking space.
   8| d. The width of a driveway for one-way use shall be a minimum of ten (10) feet and for two-
   9| way use shall be a minimum of twenty (20) feet and a maximum of thirty (30) feet, as meas-
  10| ured at the setback line.
  11| e. Where access or egress is provided for a parking lot, or one or more loading bays, such ac-
  12| cess or egress shall be so arranged to provide a circulation system or maneuvering space on
  13| the lot so that all vehicles may exit from and enter onto a public street by being driven in a
  14| forward direction and no vehicle shall be required to enter or leave by backing and no vehicle
  15| shall have to stand within a street right-of-way waiting to enter the lot.
  16| 2. Number of compact car spaces. In parking lots containing more than 20 spaces, not more than
  17| 33% of such spaces may be designed for use by compact cars. Such compact car spaces shall be
  18| located in one or more continuous areas and shall not be intermixed with spaces designed for
  19| standard cars and shall be clearly designated by signs or pavement marking. In parking lots with
  20| 20 or fewer parking spaces, spaces designed for use by compact cars are not permitted.
  21| 3. Loading bays. All required loading bays must have minimum dimensions as follows: 30 feet
  22| long, 12 feet wide and 14 feet high. Each loading bay shall have a maneuvering space equal to
  23| its length. Where the long portion of a loading bay abuts a wall, column or other obstacle, or in
  24| other cases where the permitting authority requests, evidence shall be provided that the loading
  25| bay and its maneuvering space are adequate to accommodate large motor vehicles and trailers.
  26| All required loading bays shall have an adequate travel isle to provide access. This may be sepa-
  27| rately located or through the parking area. In either instance the “Truck Route” shall be clearly
  28| delineated by signage or pavement marking. An adequate travel path shall provide width and
  29| turning radii appropriate to a WB-55 or the largest truck expected to deliver goods to the site,
  30| whichever is larger.
  31| 4. Marking. In a parking lot or loading area, the surface of the parking lot or loading area shall
  32| be painted, marked or otherwise delineated so that the location of the parking spaces and load-
  33| ing bays is apparent, and signs shall be erected indicating that loading bays, and, if necessary,
  34| compact or other reserved parking spaces, are reserved for such use. Where 50% or more of the
  35| required parking spaces in a parking lot are assigned, such as to individual employees or to
  36| dwelling units in a dwelling, parking spaces for guests or visitors to the use or establishment,
  37| not to exceed 10% of the required parking spaces, shall be located and designated, by signage or
  38| pavement marking, as visitor parking near the principal entrance to the building which they
  39| serve.

===PAGE 161=== (plain, 38 lines)
   1| 161
   2| Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   3| 5. Availability. To ensure the availability and utilization of required parking spaces and loading
   4| bays on a year-round basis:
   5| a. Unless authorized by Special Permit or Site Plan Approval, no fee or other charge to the
   6| parker, in addition to a lease or purchase agreement applicable to occupants generally, shall
   7| be made for a parking space or loading bay required to serve a use, building, or establish-
   8| ment.
   9| b. Each required off-street parking space and loading bay shall be designed so that any motor
  10| vehicle may proceed to and from said space without requiring the moving of any other vehi-
  11| cle or by passing over any other space or bay.
  12| c. Parking spaces for vehicles larger than automobiles, such as large trucks or buses, shall be
  13| specifically identified on the off-street parking and loading plan and shall be of such dimen-
  14| sion as to accommodate the specified type of vehicle. Such vehicles shall be permitted to
  15| park only in the spaces so identified and approved.
  16| 6. Snow storage. An aggregate area equal to, at a minimum, ten percent (10%) of the paved area
  17| shall be set aside for snow storage. Snow storage shall be designated, at a minimum, in two sep-
  18| arate and distinct places on the property. These areas shall be located on the submitted off-street
  19| parking and loading plan.
  20| In addition snow storage shall be prohibited from:
  21|  a. Being located on/in stormwater structures or ponds;
  22|  b. Impacting the sight lines at intersections both within the site and where the site driveway
  23| meets public roads.
  24|  c. Being located in/on off-street parking spaces that are required per the Protective Bylaw.
  25| 7. Surfacing and drainage.
  26| a. All required parking spaces and loading bays, maneuvering aisles, and driveways shall
  27| have a durable, dustless, all-weather surface suitable for year-round use, such as asphalt or
  28| concrete, and shall dispose of surface water by grading and drainage in such a manner that no
  29| surface water shall drain onto any public way or onto any lot in other ownership.
  30| b. It is the intent of this Section that the paved surface of a parking lot or loading area shall be lim-
  31| ited to such areas as are necessary for the parking spaces, loading bays, maneuvering aisles, and
  32| driveways required to meet the provisions of this Section. The off-street parking and loading plan
  33| required by this Section shall demonstrate that all paved areas associated with a parking lot are nec-
  34| essary for the storing, standing, or maneuvering of vehicles; the permitting authority may deny the
  35| request for a permit when more area is paved than is necessary to comply with the provisions of this
  36| Section.
  37| 8. Grade. The maximum grade of any required maneuvering aisle, parking space, or loading bay
  38| shall be 10%.

===PAGE 162=== (layout, 37 lines)
   1|                                      Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   2|     9. Landscaping.
   3|         a. On at least three sides of the perimeter of an outdoor parking lot containing 20 or more
   4|         parking spaces, there must be at least one tree for every eight parking spaces abutting the
   5|         perimeter; such trees must be spaced so that some part of a parking space is not more than
   6|         30 feet from the center line of a tree.
   7|         b. In the interior part of an outdoor parking lot where two rows of parking spaces containing
   8|         a total of 10 or more parking spaces face each other, a landscaped open space not less than
   9|         five feet in width must be provided. The landscaped strip may be provided either:
  10|              i. Between the rows of parking spaces parallel to the aisle; or
  11|              ii. In two or more strips parallel to the spaces and extending from the aisle serving one
  12|              row of spaces to the aisle serving the other row of spaces. There must be in each such
  13|              strip, at least three trees and in all such strips not fewer than one tree for every eight
  14|              parking spaces in the interior part of the parking lot. Trees must be spaced so that some
  15|              part of each parking space is not more than 30 feet from a tree.
  16|         c. Trees required by this Section shall be at least two inches in diameter at a height four feet
  17|         above the ground at time of planting and shall be of a species characterized by suitability
  18|         and hardiness for location in a parking lot. To the extent practicable, existing trees shall be
  19|         retained and used to satisfy this Section.
  20|     10. Exception for one-family or two-family dwelling. The provisions of 6.4.3 b) 5; 6.43 e) 4, 5
  21|     & 6 with regard to backing into a public street, marking of pavement, moving of vehicles and
  22|     surfacing and drainage and snow storage shall not apply where parking is provided for any one
  23|     -family or two-family dwelling.
  24|     Bicycle Parking
  25|     1. Required spaces. In any parking area with twenty or more spaces as defined in the parking
  26|     and loading tables, a minimum of two bicycle parking spaces shall be provided, and one addi-
  27|     tional bicycle parking space shall be provided for each increment of 20 motor vehicle parking
  28|     spaces over 40 vehicle spaces.
  29|     2. Placement and access. Bicycle parking shall be located near the primary entrance(s) of the
  30|     building. Half of the bicycle parking spaces shall be provided as long-term parking, safe and
  31|     secure from vandalism and theft and protected from the elements. The other half shall be pro-
  32|     vided as short-term (customer or visitor) parking, and short-term parking spaces shall be visi-
  33|     ble and convenient to the building entrance. Bicycle parking apparatus shall not be installed in
  34|     a manner that will cause obstruction of pedestrian or motor vehicle traffic. Bicycle parking
  35|     shall be situated in such a way that normal snow removal activities and snow storage do not
  36|     impact the bicycle parking facility.

   2| Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   3| 3. Dimensional Regulation. Each bicycle parking space shall be sufficient to accommodate a
   4| bicycle six feet in length and two feet in width.
   5| 4. Design. Bicycle parking apparatus shall be of a high-security design to which the frame and
   6| wheel of a parked bicycle may be attached; installed in a visible location to deter vandalism
   7| and theft; and permanently mounted to the ground or to a building or other immovable struc-
   8| ture. Inverted-U-frame or other racks that support the bicycle at two or more points above the
   9| center of gravity are required. VOTED 2/3RDS DECLARED to amend the Zoning Bylaw, Sec-

  13| ARTICLE 40. To see if the Town will vote to amend the Zoning Bylaw, Section 8.4, Develop-
  14| ment Plan Review, by deleting the entirety of the existing wording and inserting a new Section 8.4
  15| Site Plan Approval, or take any action relative thereto.
  16| (Submitted by Planning Board) Board of Selectmen and Finance Committee will make recommen-
  17| dation at town meeting.
  18| 8.4. Site Plan Approval
  19| 8.4.1. Purpose
  20| The purpose of the Site Plan Review Bylaw hereby established is to protect the safety, public
  21| health, convenience and general welfare of the inhabitants of the Town by ensuring that the design
  22| and layout of certain developments permitted by right or by Special Permit will constitute suitable
  23| developments and will not result in a detriment to the neighborhood or the environment.
  24| 8.4.2. Applicability
  25| 8.4.2.1. Projects Requiring Site Plan Review
  26| a) New construction or exterior expansion of any non-residential building or multi-family
  27| dwelling containing more than four (4) units. "Expansion" shall include a floor space in-
  28| crease of 5,000 square feet or 25% or more within any ten (10) year period, whichever is
  29| less.
  30| b) The construction or enlargement of any multi-family dwelling containing more than four (4)
  31| units, or buildings accessory to such dwellings, including such dwellings on contiguous
  32| lots under the same ownership.
  33| c) The construction, rehabilitation, of a building involving ten (10) or more parking spaces.
  34| d) The construction, reconfiguration or renovation of parking facilities, with the exception of
  35| normal maintenance.
  36| e) Any use designated that is grated a Special Permit or Variance by the Zoning Board of
  37|     Appeals.

===PAGE 164=== (plain, 37 lines)
   1| 164
   2| Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   3| f) Any change of use within an existing building or site.
   4| g) Any child care facility.
   5| 8.4.3. Procedure
   6| 8.4.3.1. Applications are to be prepared in accordance with the Planning Board Rules and Regulations
   7| for Site Plan Approval and Special Permit. These plans are to be submitted to the Planning Office in
   8| the number outlined in the aforementioned document.
   9| 8.4.3.2. Public Hearing on Site Plans
  10| a) The Planning Board shall hold a public hearing within sixty -five (65) days of the receipt of a
  11| complete application and shall take final action within ninety (90) days from the time of the
  12| hearing, as provided for in MGL Chapter 40A, Sections 9 and 11.
  13| b) The Planning Board's final action, rendered in writing, shall consist of either:
  14| 1. Approval of the site plan based upon determination that the proposed plan will constitute a
  15| suitable development and is in compliance with the standards set forth in this Bylaw; or
  16| 2. Disapproval of the site plan based upon a determination that the proposed plan does not meet
  17| the standards set forth in this Bylaw; or
  18| 3. Approval of the site plan subject to any condition, modification or restriction required by the
  19| Planning Board which will ensure that the project meets the standards set forth in this Bylaw.
  20| 8.4.3.3. Failure of the Board to take final action upon an application for Site Plan Review within the
  21| time specified above shall be deemed to be approval of said application. Upon issuance by the Town
  22| Clerk of an appropriate certification that the allowed time has passed without Planning Board action,
  23| the required building permits may be issued.
  24| 8.4.4. Site Design Principles
  25| 8.4.4.1. All Site Plan Review applicants shall adhere to the following general principles when design-
  26| ing a site plan for land within the Town of Lunenburg.
  27| a) Landscaping Within the Setbacks: Applicants are expected to design setbacks with landscaping,
  28| maintain the landscaping and replace any landscaping that has not fully established itself. Front
  29| yard setback landscaping shall consist of street trees, low -level plantings and other visually
  30| unobtrusive flora. Landscaping shall be situated in a manner such that vehicular and pedestrian
  31| sight lines are not restricted.
  32| b) Driveways and Curb Cuts: Entrance and exit location shall be designed to facilitate safe traffic
  33| movement both on and off the site as well as internally.  Curb cuts are limited to as few as
  34| practicable for the length of street frontage with adequate separation from adjacent parcels,
  35| other curb cuts and intersections.
  36| c) Service Roads/Connection of Parking Lots: To minimize turning movements onto adjacent pub-
  37| lic ways, developers are encouraged to provide internal circulation systems (service roads)

===PAGE 165=== (plain, 34 lines)
   1| 165
   2| Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   3|      that connect to adjacent developments (parking area to parking area). Site plans that pro-
   4| pose service roads and/or connection of parking areas shall show on the plan how the con-
   5| nection of parking areas will be achieved and have a deeded agreement and easement from
   6| the abutter.
   7| d)  Parking and Loading Areas:
   8| 1. Access locations shall be designed to encourage unimpeded traffic flow with controlled
   9| turning movements and minimum hazards to vehicular and pedestrian traffic.
  10| 2. Facilities and access routes for deliveries, service and maintenance shall be separated, where
  11| practical, from public access routes and parking areas.
  12| 3. Parking facilities shall be designed to prevent parked cars from damaging trees, shrubs and
  13| curbing, and shall not disrupt pedestrian walkways.
  14| 4. Walkways shall be separated from areas of vehicular traffic and designed to minimize
  15| stormwater runoff while maximizing access.
  16| 5. These facilities shall include appropriate landscaping, pedestrian access and multi -modal
  17| parking accommodations.
  18| e) Stormwater Management (Grading and Drainage): All site plans shall be designed to com-
  19| ply with DEP (Department of Environmental Protection) Stormwater Management Stand-
  20| ards. Furthermore, the use of Low Impact Development, infiltration of stormwater and re-
  21| duction of impervious surface are strongly encouraged.
  22| f) Lighting: Lighting designs shall be Dark Sky compliant.  This includes all exterior lights
  23| being designed and installed in such a manner as to prevent objectionable light at (and
  24| glare across) the property lines.  Developments shall eliminate glare onto adjacent proper-
  25| ties through the use of lighting shields, earthen berms, addition of new landscaping and/or
  26| retention of existing natural vegetation.
  27| 8.4.5. Standards for Site Plan Review
  28| 8.4.5.1. In reviewing a site plan application, the Planning Board shall take the following items into
  29| consideration:
  30| a) Compliance with all applicable provisions of the Lunenburg Protective Bylaw; Planning
  31| Board Rules and Regulations for Site Plan Approval and Special Permit; and Subdivision
  32| Regulations.
  33| b) Traffic safety and ease of access at street and highway entrances and exits, to be determined
  34| by

===PAGE 166=== (plain, 30 lines)
   1| 166
   2| Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   3| 1. The adequacy of grades,
   4|        2. The presence of sight distances that are appropriate for the design speed of the road, as
   5|            determined  by ASHTO,
   6|        3. Minimum safe distance between such exits and entrances, and
   7|  4. The existence of proper alignment or safe distance from proximal existing street(s) and drive
   8|       way entrances.
   9| c) Safety and adequacy of driveway layout and pedestrian walkways; off-street parking areas; off-
  10| street loading areas for materials and products; to be determined by
  11| 1. Adequate access for service and emergency vehicles,
  12| 2. Proper separation of pedestrian and vehicular facilities,
  13| 3. The presence of separated facilities for delivery of goods, and
  14| 4. Acceptable traffic patterns to/from the site entrance/exit and within the parking facilities.
  15| d) Safe and adequate means of disposal of sewage, garbage and rubbish.
  16| e) Safe and adequate water supply and distribution; including sufficient water and facilities for
  17| firefighting on the site.
  18|   f) Safe and adequate storm drainage consistent with
  19|            1. building and surface coverage,
  20|            2. grade and slopes,
  21|           3. soils and
  22|           4. water table
  23| All of which shall result in zero increase in the rate of runoff from the site, as measured by the 2-Year
  24| (24-hour) and 10-Year (24-hour) Storm Event Standards; and there will be no negative impacts to
  25| downstream property-owners in a 100-year (24-hour) storm event. The stormwater system shall meet
  26| all the requirements and standards of the Massachusetts Stormwater Management Standards, as
  27| amended.
  28| g) Prevention of soil erosion during and after construction; provisions for an increase in the vol-
  29| ume of runoff of surface water from the site and the protection of adjacent areas against detri-
  30| mental or offensive uses on the site by the provision of adequate buffers against light, sight,

===PAGE 167=== (layout, 37 lines)
   1|                                      Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   2|     sound, dust and vibrations.
   3|        h) Open space provisions and landscaping, including the maximum retention of on-site natu-
   4|          ral features.
   5|        i)  Placement of underground utilities, night lighting and signs.
   6|        j) Compatibility of soil and subsoil's to type and intensity of development.
   7|        k) No exterior lighting shall shine directly on adjacent properties, or in such a manner as to
   8|          create a nuisance on such properties or a hazard on public ways. To ensure exterior lighting
   9|          conforms to this provision the following conditions shall be met.
  10|          1.  For the proposed use all lighting installed shall conform to the standards outlined in The
  11|          Illuminating Engineering Society of North America Lighting Manual.
  12|          2.  All lighting, with the exception of security lighting, shall be turned on no earlier than one
  13|          half hour prior to the business opening and turned off no later than one half hour after the
  14|          business closing.

  16|          sions” section of the Dark Sky Society’s 2009                  Guidelines for Good Exterior Lighting
  17|          Plans, as amended;
  18|          4. All fixtures using LED lighting shall be in the range of “warm-white” or shall be fil-

  20|     1) Snow storage areas shall be shown on the site plan and shall not interfere with sight distanc-
  21|          es at points of ingress/egress to a site or pedestrian/vehicle circulation, nor shall it adverse-
  22|          ly impact surrounding water bodies, streams, wetlands, or other resource areas as defined
  23|          in Chapter 131 section 40 Massachusetts General Laws, and the Lunenburg Wetlands By-
  24|          law, as amended.
  25| 8.4.6. Modifications to an Approved Site Plan
  26| 8.4.6.1. Once a site plan has been approved by the Planning Board, said plan shall not be changed,
  27| amended or modified without approval of the Board. There shall be only one final site plan in ef-
  28| fect for a parcel of land at a time.
  29| 8.4.7. Construction of an Approved Site Plan
  30| 8.4.7.1. Construction on a site with an approved site plan must be started within one (1) year from
  31| the date of the Planning Board's approval of the site plan. Site plan approval may be extended for
  32| one (1) year at the discretion of the Board after receipt of a written request from the landowner,
  33| showing good cause. If one year has elapsed from the date of approval; and no extensions have
  34| been granted; or if one year has elapsed since the granting of said extension; the final site plan ap-
  35| proval shall become null and void without requiring any further action by the Planning Board.
  36| This time period shall not include delays resulting from litigation.
  37|                                                             167

===PAGE 168=== (plain, 35 lines)
   1| 168
   2| Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   3| 8.4.7.2. No permit to build, alter or expand any building or structure, or change of use requiring Site
   4| Plan Review under this Bylaw shall be issued by the Building Inspector; nor shall any construction
   5| commence before a written statement of Approval or Approval with Conditions has been issued by
   6| the Planning Board.
   7| 8.4.7.3. The Building Inspector may inspect a site under construction for compliance with the ap-
   8| proved site plan.
   9| 8.4.8. Enforcement
  10| 8.4.8.1. It shall be the duty of the Building Inspector/Zoning Enforcement Officer to administer and
  11| enforce the provisions of this Bylaw.
  12| 8.4.8.2. The Planning Board shall provide a schedule of inspection fees to be paid by the applicant
  13| prior to the start of construction.
  14| 8.4.8.3. The Planning Board may use inspection fees paid by the applicant to offset the cost of hir-
  15| ing any additional engineers or inspectors necessary to monitor all phases of construction covered
  16| by an approved site plan.
  17| 8.4.8.4. If work is not performed per the approved plan, including all conditions, the Building In-
  18| spector/Zoning Enforcement Officer has the authority to issue a cease and desist order per the Plan-
  19| ning Board Rules and Regulations for Site Plan Approval and Special Permits, as amended.
  20| 8.4.8.5. "As Built" plans, certified by a registered professional and noting any change from the ap-
  21| proved plan, shall be filed with the Building Inspector and the Planning Board before a Certificate
  22| of Completion shall be issued.
  23| 8.4.8.6. Building Inspector shall issue a Certificate of Completion when all construction has been
  24| performed and all other requirements have been met in compliance with the approved site plan. A
  25| copy of the Certificate of Completion will be filed with the Planning Board.
  26| 8.4.8.7. No Certificate of Occupancy shall be issued for any structure or use subject to site plan re-
  27| view unless a Certificate of Completion has been issued.
  28| 8.4.8.8. The Building Inspector may issue a Temporary Certificate of Occupancy for a period of one
  29| (1) year if the required construction has been substantially completed and the permitted uses of the
  30| development can be carried on in a safe and convenient manner.
  31| 8.4.9. Rules and Regulations
  32| 8.4.9.1. The Planning Board may adopt such rules and regulations for carrying out its duties under
  33| this Section. The Board may, where such action allowed by law, in the public interest and not in-
  34| consistent with the purpose and intent of this Bylaw, waive strict compliance with any requirement
  35| of this Site Plan Review Bylaw or its rules and regulations.

   6| CHANGES new language SHOWN IN strikethrough
   7| SECTION 2:  JURISDICTION ~ Except as permitted by the Conservation Commission or as pro-
   8| vided in this bylaw, no person shall commence to remove, fill, dredge, build upon, degrade, dis-
   9| charge into, or otherwise alter the following resource areas: any freshwater wetlands; marshes;
  10| wet meadows; bogs; swamps; vernal pools; banks; reservoirs; lakes; ponds of any size; creeks;
  11| beaches; lands under water bodies; lands subject to flooding or inundation by groundwater or sur-
  12| face water; lands subject to flooding; lands within 100 feet of any of the aforesaid resource areas;
  13| perennial rivers and streams and lands within 200 feet of such rivers and streams (collectively the
  14| "resource areas protected by this bylaw"). Said resource areas shall be protected whether or not
  15| they border surface waters. (ADD THE FOLLOWING SENTENCE TO THIS SECTION) Lands
  16| within 100 feet of aforesaid resource areas and within 200 feet of rivers or perennial streams lo-
  17| cated within the corporate boundary of Lunenburg shall be protected regardless of the location of
  18| the wetland with respect to municipal corporate boundary.
  19| SECTION 5: NOTICE OF HEARINGS ~ Any person filing a permit application or (REMOVE
  20| RFD AND ADD) RDA with the Commission at the same time shall give written notice thereof, by
  21| certified mail (return receipt requested), (ADD) certificate of mail or hand delivery, to all abutters
  22| according to the most recent applicable tax list of the assessors, including owners of land directly
  23| opposite on any public or private street or way, including any in another municipality or across a
  24| body of water. The notice to abutters shall enclose a copy of the permit application or request,
  25| with plans, or shall state where copies may be examined and obtained by abutters. An affidavit of
  26| the person providing such notice, with a copy of the notice mailed or delivered, shall be filed with
  27| the Commission. When a person requesting a determination is other than the owner, the request,
  28| the notice of the hearing and the determination itself shall be sent by the Commission to the owner
  29| as well as to the person making the request.
  30| The Commission shall conduct a public hearing on any application or (REMOVE RFD AND
  31| ADD) RDA, with written notice given at the expense of the applicant, five business days prior to
  32| the hearing, in a newspaper of general circulation in the municipality.
  33| The Commission shall commence the public hearing within 21 days from receipt of a completed
  34| application or request for determination unless an extension is authorized in writing by the appli-
  35| cant.
  36| The Commission shall issue its permit or determination in writing within 21 days of the close of
  37| the public hearing thereon unless an extension is authorized in writing by the applicant.
  38| The Commission in an appropriate case may combine its hearing under this bylaw with the hear-
  39| ing conducted under the Wetlands Protection Act, G.L. Ch.131, s. 40, and Regulations, 310 CMR
  40| 10.00.
  41|                                                           171

===PAGE 172=== (plain, 42 lines)
   1| 172
   2| Annual Town Meeting – May 5, 2016
   3| The Commission shall have authority to continue the hearing, (ADD) with the applicant’s consent
   4| written or granted verbally on the record during the course of the public hearing or meeting, to a
   5| certain date announced at the hearing, for reasons stated at the hearing, which may include receipt
   6| of additional information from the applicant or others deemed necessary by the Commission in its
   7| discretion, or comments and recommendations of boards and officials listed in Section 6.
   8| SECTION 6: COORDINATION WITH OTHER BOARDS ~ Any person filing a Wetlands Protec-
   9| tion Act Permit application or (REMOVE RFD AND ADD) RDA with the Commission shall pro-
  10| vide written notice thereof at the same time, by certified mail or hand delivery, to the Board of"
  11| Selectmen, Planning Board, Zoning Board of Appeals, Board of Health and Building Inspector.
  12| The Commission shall not take final action until such boards and officials have had fourteen (14)
  13| days from receipt of notice to file written comments and recommendations with the Commission,
  14| which the Commission shall take into account, but which shall not be binding on the Commission.
  15| The applicant shall have the right to receive any such comments and recommendations, and to re-
  16| spond to them at a hearing of the Commission, prior to final action.
  17| SECTION 7: PERMITS AND CONDITIONS
  18| (CHANGE TO PARAGRAPH 6)
  19| A permit shall expire three years from the date of issuance. Notwithstanding the above, the Com-
  20| mission in its discretion may issue a permit expiring five years from the date of issuance for recur-
  21| ring or continuous maintenance work, provided that annual notification of time and location of
  22| work is given to the Commission. (ADD) The Commission may extend a permit or a previous ex-
  23| tension for an additional period of no greater than three years per extension. Any permit may be
  24| renewed once for an additional one year period, provided that a request for a renewal is received in
  25| writing by the Commission prior to expiration. Notwithstanding the above, a permit may contain
  26| requirements which shall be enforceable for a stated number of years, indefinitely, or until perma-
  27| nent protection is in place, and shall apply to all owners of the land.
  28| SECTION 8. REGULATIONS~ After public notice and public hearing, the Commission shall
  29| promulgate rules and regulations to effectuate the purposes of this bylaw. Failure by the Commis-
  30| sion to promulgate such rules and regulations or a legal declaration of their invalidity by a court of
  31| law shall not3 act to suspend or invalidate the effect of this bylaw.
  32| At a minimum these regulations shall define key terms in this bylaw not inconsistent with the by-
  33| law and procedures governing the amount and filing of fees. (ADD THE FOLLOWING PARA-
  34| GRAPH) The Conservation Commission will establish a draft of proposed changes during regular,
  35| properly posted open meetings. Once there is consensus on the proposed changes, a formal hear-
  36| ing will be scheduled and will take place over 2 regularly scheduled meetings.  Both hearings will
  37| be televised.   A notice of the hearings will be put in a local newspaper at least 10 business days
  38| prior to the hearing dates.  At the discretion of the Conservation Commission, the hearing may be
  39| continued to the next consecutive open, regularly scheduled meeting. Once the hearing is closed,
  40| the final changes will be drafted and voted on. The proposed and approved changes will be posted
  41| on the Commission’s Town website. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY to amend the General Bylaws of
  42| the Town, Section XXI, General Wetlands Bylaw, as printed in the warrant.
